Secondary Homological Stability for Unordered Configuration Spaces Thanks: Zachary Himes was supported in part by a Frederick N. Andrews Fellowship, Department of Mathematics, Purdue University.
Zachary Himes

Abstract
. Secondary homological stability is a recently discovered stability pattern for a sequence of spaces exhibiting homological stability and it holds outside the range where the homology stabilizes. We prove secondary homological stability for the homology of the unordered configuration spaces of a connected manifold. The main difficulty is the case that the manifold is closed because there are no obvious maps inducing stability and the homology eventually is periodic instead of stable. We resolve this issue by constructing a new chain-level stabilization map for configuration spaces.



1.
Introduction

The unordered configuration space of
k
points in a manifold
M
is the quotient space

\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in M^{k}:x_{i}\neq x_{j}\text{ if }i\neq j\}/\Sigma_{k}.

The subset
\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in M^{k}:x_{i}\neq x_{j}\text{ if }i\neq j\}\subset M^{k}
has the subspace topology and the symmetric group
\Sigma_{k}
acts on it by permuting the order of the coordinates.
In this paper, we study stability patterns for the homology groups
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
for a connected topological manifold
M
. When the homology is over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
, we prove that there are two stability patterns, depending on whether the manifold is open
or closed. When the manifold is open, the homology of the configuration space exhibits secondary homological stability, in the sense of
[
GKR19
, Theorem A]
. When the manifold is closed, the homology of the configuration space has a new pattern called periodic secondary homological stability.

1.1.
Homological Stability

Given an embedding

\iota\colon L\sqcup M\hookrightarrow P,

there is an induced map

\text{Conf}(\iota)\colon\text{Conf}_{j}(L)\times\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\to\text{Conf}_{j+k}(P).

By the Künneth theorem,
there is an induced map

\displaystyle\text{Conf}(\iota)_{*}\colon\bigoplus_{i=a+b}H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{j}(L);R)\otimes H_{b}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)

\displaystyle\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{j+k}(P);R)

\displaystyle\omega\otimes\tau

\displaystyle\mapsto\text{Conf}(\iota)_{*}(\omega\otimes\tau).

Fixing a homology class
x\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{j}(L);R)
and a representative cycle
x^{\prime}\in C_{a}(\text{Conf}_{j}(L);R)
of
x
yields a map

\displaystyle t_{x}\colon H_{b}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)

\displaystyle\to H_{a+b}(\text{Conf}_{j+k}(P);R)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\text{Conf}(\iota)_{*}(\omega\otimes x^{\prime}).

When the manifold
M
is a non-compact and without boundary (we will call such manifolds
open manifolds
), there is an embedding
\iota\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\sqcup M\to M
(see e.g.
[
KM15
, Lemma 2.4]
). In this case, when
x\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
is the class of a point, the map,

t_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{x}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R),

which depends on
the choice of an end of
M
,
is known as the
stabilization map
(see
Figure

1
).

Figure 1.
The stabilization map
t_{1}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M))\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M))

In
[
McD75
, p. 104]
, McDuff proved that the stabilization map
t_{1}
is an isomorphism in integral homology
for all
k\gg i
if
M
is a smooth manifold of finite type. The isomorphism
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\cong H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)
for all
k\gg i
is known as
homological stability
. The
stable range
is the range where
t_{1}
is an isomorphism and has been explicitly computed for homology with various coefficients
(the current best known ranges for various coefficients are
[
Seg79
, Proposition A.1]
,
[
Chu12
, Corollary 3]
,
[
Ran13a
, Theorem B]
,
[
KM15
, Theorem 1.4]
,
[
Knu17
, Theorem 1.3]
, and
Proposition

3.6
, where we improve the stable range for odd torsion when the manifold is a surface). Since the stabilization map
t_{1}
is also induced from the map of chain complexes

\displaystyle C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)

\displaystyle\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\mapsto\text{Conf}(\iota)_{*}(\xi\otimes x)

one can make sense of relative homology. The stable range is the same as the range where the relative homology
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M),\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
vanishes because the stabilization map
t_{1}
is injective by
[
McD75
, p. 103]
.

1.2.
Secondary Homological Stability

If we replace
x\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
with another homology class
z\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
we get a new map of homology groups

\displaystyle t_{z}\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)

\displaystyle\to H_{*+a}(\text{Conf}_{k+b}(M);R).

It is natural to ask if this map yields a different stability pattern for
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
.
In general, the map
t_{z}\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to H_{*+a}(\text{Conf}_{k+b}(M);R)
is not an isomorphism in a range for any choice of
z
because it misses some classes in the image of
t_{1}
. But, we will show that
t_{z}
induces a map of relative homology groups

t_{z}\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);R)\to H_{*+a}(\text{Conf}_{k+b}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1+b}(M);R).

This induced map of relative homology groups will be an isomorphism in a new range for certain choices of
z\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
.
In the range that
the homology
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)

stabilizes, the relative homology groups
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);R)
and
H_{*+a}(\text{Conf}_{k+b}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1+b}(M);R)
are isomorphic because both groups are zero. In this paper, we will show that the map
t_{z}
is an isomorphism in a range where the relative homology groups are potentially nonzero.

Isomorphisms of the form

H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);R)\cong H_{*+a}(\text{Conf}_{k+b}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1+b}(M);R)

are called
secondary homological stability
. We prove the following secondary homological stability result (as well as higher-order stability results-see
Corollary

3.13
and
Proposition

3.17
) for the configuration spaces of an open manifold.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
M
be an open connected manifold of dimension
n
. Let
R
be either
\mathbb{Z}
or the field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Let
g(R,n,k)
denote the constant

g(R,n,k)=\begin{cases}\frac{p^{2}-1}{p^{2}}k-\frac{2p^{2}-2p-2}{p^{2}}&\text{if }R=\mathbb{F}_{p}\text{ for }p\text{ an odd prime}\text{ and }n=2\\
(2/3)k&\text{if }R=\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }n=2\\
(3/4)k&\text{if }R=\mathbb{F}_{2}\text{ or if }n>2\text{ and }R=\mathbb{Z}.\\
\end{cases}

There exists a homology class
z\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
with

(a,b)=\begin{cases}(2p-2,2p)&\text{if }R=\mathbb{F}_{p}\text{ for }p\text{ an odd prime and }n=2\\
(1,2)&\text{if }R=\mathbb{F}_{2}\text{ or if }R=\mathbb{Z}.\\
\end{cases}

such that the stabilization map

t_{z}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);R)\to H_{i+a}(\text{Conf}_{k+b}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1+b}(M);R)

is an isomorphism for
i<g(R,n,k)
and a surjection for
i=g(R,n,k)
.

The ranges in this theorem are optimal—they are achieved when
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. All of the proofs in this paper assume that
n\geq 2
but many of the statements are vacuously true when
n=1
.
The assumption that
n=2
when
p
is odd in
Theorem

1.1
may be dropped. But in the case
n\neq 2
and
p
is odd, the relative homology groups
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})
vanish in a range including the above range by
[
KM15
, Proposition 3.3]
. Hence, these groups do not exhibit nontrivial secondary stability in this range.
When the manifold
M
is a surface and
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
for
p
an odd prime, we also show that the relative homology
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);R)
vanishes for
i\leq\frac{k(p-1)-(p-2)}{p}
and that this range is optimal (
Proposition

3.6
).

The homology classes in
Theorem

1.1
are explicitly given in
Section

3
. The case
R=\mathbb{F}_{2}
or
\mathbb{Z}
is a folk theorem known to some experts and follows from calculations in
[
BCT89
]
. When the ring
R
is
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, a picture for the homology class
z
is given by two points “doing a half twist” (see
Figure

2
).

Figure 2.
The secondary stabilization map for homology with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients

This class is similar to the class in
[
MW19
]
—in their case, the homology class inducing secondary representation stability for ordered configuration spaces is given by two ordered points doing a full twist. When the ring
R
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}
for
p
an odd prime, the picture for
z
is a
(2p-2)
-parameter family of
2p
spinning points and hence is harder to illustrate.

The first example of secondary homological stability is in
[
GKR19
, Theorem A]
which states that the relative homology of the mapping class groups of a surface of genus
g
with one boundary component
H_{i}(\text{Mod}_{g,1},\text{Mod}_{g-1,1};\mathbb{Z})
stabilizes as the genus
g
increases by
3
and the homological degree
i
increases by
2
. A secondary stability result in the setting of representation stability for the ordered configuration space of an open connected manifold of dimension at least
2
can be found in
[
MW19
, Theorem 1.2]
. For other secondary stability results, see also
[
MPP
, Theorem B]
,
[
BG23
, Theorem C]
,
[
Hep20
, Theorems B and C]
, and
[
Ho
]
.

1.3.
Periodic Secondary Homological Stability

We now discuss similar results when the manifold is a compact manifold without boundary (we will call such manifolds
closed manifolds
).
When
M
is a closed manifold there is no obvious stabilization map
\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\to\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M)
.
Moreover, direct calculations show that the homology
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
does not stabilize in general—for example,
H_{1}(\text{Conf}_{k}(S^{2});\mathbb{Z})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/(2k-2)\mathbb{Z}
for
k\geq 2
by a presentation of the spherical braid group given in
[
FV62
, p. 255]
.

Even though the integral homology does not stabilize in general, if instead one considers homology with coefficients over a different ring
R
, then there are still stability patterns for
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
. For example, if the manifold
M
is orientable and the ring
R
is
\mathbb{Q}
,
[
Chu12
, Corollary 3]
states that
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{Q})
is isomorphic to
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{Q})
for
i<k
. Randal-Williams and Knudsen later on showed that the assumption that
M
is orientable can be dropped (
[
Ran13a
, Theorem C]
and
[
Knu17
, Theorem 1.3]
). In the same paper, Randal-Williams also showed that
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})
is isomorphic to
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})
for
i\leq k/2
.
Nagpal (and independently
[
CP15
, Theorem D]
) proved that if the ring
R
is the field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
p
an odd prime, then
under various conditions on
M
, the homology
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})
is periodic with period a power of
p
in an explicit range (
[
Nag15
, Theorem F]
). Currently, the strongest periodicity result is
[
KM16
, Theorem A]
which states that when the dimension of
M
is even and
p
is an odd prime, the homology
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})
is isomorphic to
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+p}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})
in an explicit range
and when the dimension of
M
is odd,
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{Z})
is isomorphic to
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{Z})
for
i<k/2
. For other stability patterns in the homology of configuration spaces, see
[
BM14
, Theorem 4.7]
,
[
CP15
]
, and
[
NS18
, Theorem 5.1]
.

We prove a secondary stability result (as well as higher-order stability results—see
Proposition

6.13
) for configuration spaces of closed manifolds. Since an embedding
M\sqcup\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M
does not exist when
M
is a closed manifold, it is not obvious what relative homology should even mean. Although Kupers–Miller and Randal-Williams were able to prove stability patterns for the homology groups of the configuration spaces of closed manifolds,
they only show that the homology groups are abstractly isomorphic and they do not even produce maps at the level of homology groups.
We resolve this issue by constructing new chain-level stabilization maps that yield the stability results of
[
Ran13a
, Theorem C]
and

[
KM16
, Theorem A]
.
These new stabilization maps allow us to define relative homology groups and we will also show that they induce stabilization maps of relative homology groups.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
M
be a closed connected
manifold of dimension
n
. Let
R
be either
\mathbb{Z}
or the field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

(1)

Suppose that
n
is odd or that
R
is
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. There is a class
z\in H_{1}(\text{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
and a stabilization map

t_{z,c}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);R)\to H_{i+1}(\text{Conf}_{k+2}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)

that is an isomorphism for
i<(3/4)k
and a surjection for
i=(3/4)k
.

(2)

Suppose that
n
is
2
and
R
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}
with
p>2
. There is a class
z\in H_{2p-2}(\text{Conf}_{2p}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
and a stabilization map

t_{z,c}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-p}(M);R)\to H_{i+2p-2}(\text{Conf}_{k+2p}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+p}(M);R)

that is an isomorphism for
i<\frac{p^{2}-1}{p^{2}}k-\frac{2p^{2}-2p-2}{p^{2}}
and a surjection for
i=\frac{p^{2}-1}{p^{2}}k-\frac{2p^{2}-2p-2}{p^{2}}
.

In the case that the ring
R
is
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the results of this paper mostly follow from
[
BCT89
, Theorem A and Theorem B]
(see
Remark

6.16
). In this case, Bödigheimer–Cohen–Taylor explicitly calculate
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})
for all
k
from
H_{*}(M;\mathbb{F}_{2})
.
Bödigheimer–Cohen–Taylor do not consider the relative homology groups
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})
, although it is conceivable that one could use ideas from their paper to define these groups. They do, however, compute
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})
and this computation suggests the presence of our secondary stability result
Theorem

1.2
.

1.4.
Proof Sketch of the Construction of the New Stabilization Map

The hardest part of the paper is constructing the stabilization map for the configuration space of a closed manifold (as opposed to proving that the map is an isomorphism) so we sketch this construction.

Let
M
be a manifold of dimension
n
and fix an open disk
D^{n}\subset M
. There is a map

\text{Conf}_{j}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\times\text{Conf}_{k}(D^{n})\to\text{Conf}_{j+k}(M).

If this map were a weak homotopy equivalence, then a stabilization map for
\text{Conf}_{j+k}(M)
could be constructed by using the stabilization map on
\text{Conf}_{k}(D^{n})
since
D^{n}
is open. Clearly this is not the case. But a similar map is a weak homotopy equivalence and similar ideas will let us construct the desired stabilization map.
We work with the functor

\displaystyle\text{Conf}\colon\text{Top}

\displaystyle\to\text{Top}

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigsqcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\text{Conf}_{k}(N)

instead of the functor
\text{Conf}_{k}
since the former has better algebraic and functorial properties than the latter
(for example,
\text{Conf}(M\sqcup N)\cong\text{Conf}(M)\times\text{Conf}(N)
).
There are maps

\displaystyle\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\times\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\to\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n}),

\displaystyle\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})

\displaystyle\to\text{Conf}(D^{n})

coming from embeddings

\displaystyle(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\sqcup(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow M\setminus\bar{D}^{n},

\displaystyle(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\sqcup D^{n}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow D^{n},

respectively. The configuration space
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
is a monoid up to coherent homotopy by stacking cylinders from end to end (see
Figure

3
).
In addition, the configuration spaces
\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})

and
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
are modules up to coherent homotopy over
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
(see
Figure

3
).

Figure 3.
The
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
-module structure of
\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})
and the monoid map for
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})

The map

\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})\to\text{Conf}(M)

is still not a weak homotopy equivalence. But, by using the
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
-module maps on
\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})
and
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
we now have two new maps

\displaystyle\bigg(\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\times\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\bigg)\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})

\displaystyle\to\text{ Conf}(M),

\displaystyle\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\times\bigg(\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})\bigg)

\displaystyle\to\text{ Conf}(M).

Due to these maps being homotopic (see
Figure

4
) and the higher homotopy coherence between these maps,

Figure 4.
\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\times\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})\to\text{Conf}(M)

there is an induced map on the derived tensor product of
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
-modules

\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\bigotimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\text{Conf}(D^{n})\to\text{Conf}(M).

Our model for the derived tensor product
is the two-sided bar construction of the modules
\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})
and
\text{Conf}(D^{n})

(see
Definition

4.9
).
By excision for factorization homology (
[
AF15
, Lemma 3.18]
, or see
Corollary

4.19
), this induced map
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
Therefore, to construct a chain-level stabilization map for
\text{Conf}(M)
with coefficients in a ring
R
, it suffices to construct a chain-level stabilization map

C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)

that is also a module map over the differential graded ring
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R});R)
.

We build such a map by giving a length two resolution of the differential graded module

C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)

by free modules over the differential graded ring
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R});R)
.
Up to homotopy equivalence, the generator can be taken to be a point in
H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(D^{n});R)
. The only relation in our presentation comes from the fact that the natural inclusion
\text{Conf}_{1}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow\text{Conf}_{1}(D^{n})
is nullhomotopic (see
Remark

4.27
for a conceptual reason for the existence of such a small resolution).
Using this presentation for the module
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)
, we can easily check when a homology class
z\in H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(D^{n});R)
gives a chain-level stabilization map

t_{z}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)

of
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R});R)
-modules: the Browder bracket of the homology class
z
and the class of a point
e\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(D^{n});R)
needs to be zero. This condition on the Browder bracket was key to the proofs of stability patterns in

[
Ran13a
]
and

[
KM16
]
, and our construction can be viewed as a categorification of their work.

Using this presentation of
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)
, we obtain a stabilization map

C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)

which is also a map of
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R});R)
-modules. Such a stabilization map induces a well-defined chain map

C_{*}\big(\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\bigotimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\text{Conf}(D^{n});R\big)\to C_{*}\big(\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\bigotimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\text{Conf}(D^{n});R\big).

This chain map on the derived tensor product gives a stabilization map

C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)

since
\text{Conf}(M\setminus\bar{D}^{n})\bigotimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\text{Conf}(D^{n})
is weakly equivalent to
\text{Conf}(M)
. Then, we can specialize to construct stabilization maps

C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k+p}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})

when
n
is even and
p
is odd, and

C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})

when
n
is odd or when
p=2
.
Moreover, the same ideas allow for the construction of secondary and higher stabilization maps.

1.5.
Outline

In
Section

2
, we review homology operations over an
E_{n}
-algebra and use Cohen–Lada–May’s calculation of
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
to get bounds for homological stability and secondary homological stability. In
Section

3
, we review homological stability and prove secondary homological stability for homology with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
coefficients of the configuration spaces of an open manifold.
In
Section

4
, we construct semi-simplicial models for
\text{Conf}(M)
and
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
.
We construct new
stabilization maps for the configuration spaces of manifolds in
Section

5
and prove periodic secondary homological stability in
Section

6
.

1.6.

Section

5.1
and for
describing when classes in
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
lift to classes in
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
. I thank Oscar Randal-Williams for suggestions on how to simplify the proofs of
Corollary

4.19
and for other helpful conversations. I also would like to thank Nicholas Wawrykow and Jennifer Wilson for feedback on an early draft of this paper. I thank the anonymous referee for their detailed feedback and for their suggestion on how to simplify the proof of
Proposition

4.26
.

2.
Homology Operations and Stability for
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

We begin this section by reviewing homology operations and some of their properties for the homology of an algebra over the little
n
-disk operad
E_{n}
(in this paper,
E_{n}
means just the little
n
-disk operad, instead of an operad homotopy equivalent to the little
n
-disk operad). Pick a homeomorphism
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to D^{n}
.
Since the unordered configuration space of points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

is an example of an
E_{n}
-algebra, there are homology operations and these homology operations are important in Cohen–Lada–May’s calculation of
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
.
We use this calculation to obtain the stable ranges for homological stability and secondary homological stability for
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. In
Section

3
and
Section

6
, we use these stable ranges to prove stability results for the configuration space of a manifold.

For simplicity, suppose that a space
A=\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}A_{k}
is an
E_{n}
-algebra. We think of
A
as being graded over
\mathbb{N}
.
Since
A
is an
E_{n}
-algebra, it has the following operations (for more information about these operations, see
[
CLM76
, p. 213–219]
and
[
GKR
, p. 158–164]
).

(1)

For chains with coefficients in a ring
R
: a multiplication map

\displaystyle-\bullet-\colon C_{q}(A_{j};R)\otimes C_{r}(A_{k};R)\to C_{q+r}(A_{j+k};R).

(2)

For chains with coefficients in a ring
R
: a Browder bracket

\displaystyle\left[-,-\right]\colon C_{q}(A_{j};R)\otimes C_{r}(A_{k};R)\to C_{q+r+n-1}(A_{j+k};R).

(3)

For homology with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
coefficients: when
p
is odd and
q+n-1
is even, there are “top" homology operations

\xi\colon H_{q}(A_{k};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{pq+(n-1)(p-1)}(A_{pk};\mathbb{F}_{p}),

\beta\xi\colon H_{q}(A_{k};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{pq+(n-1)(p-1)-1}(A_{pk};\mathbb{F}_{p}),

and an associated operation

\zeta\colon H_{q}(A_{k};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{pq+(n-1)(p-1)-1}(A_{pk};\mathbb{F}_{p}).

When
p
is
2
, the top homology operation
\xi
is defined for all
q
.

(4)

For homology with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
coefficients:
when
p
is odd, there are Dyer-Lashof operations

\displaystyle Q^{s}\colon H_{q}(A_{k};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{q+2s(p-1)}(A_{pk};\mathbb{F}_{p})

\displaystyle\text{defined for }s\geq 0\text{ with }2s-q<n-1,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\beta Q^{s}\colon H_{q}(A_{k};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{q+2s(p-1)-1}(A_{pk};\mathbb{F}_{p})

\displaystyle\text{defined for }s\geq 0\text{ with }2s-q<n-1.

When
p
is
2
, there are Dyer-Lashof operations

\displaystyle Q^{s}\colon H_{q}(A_{k};\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H_{q+s}(A_{2k};\mathbb{F}_{2})

\displaystyle\text{defined for }s\geq 0\text{ with }s-q<n-1.

When
p=2
and
s<q
, the Dyer-Lashof operation
Q^{s}
is the zero map, and when
p=2
and
s=q
, the Dyer-Lashof operation
Q^{s}
is the squaring map
Q^{s}(x)=x^{2}
(when
p
is an odd prime number, a similar description of the Dyer-Lashof operations holds for
s\leq 2q
instead of
s\leq q
).

Since we are working with spaces, the operations
\beta\xi
and
\beta Q^{s}
are the top homology operation
\xi
and the Dyer-Lashof operations
Q^{s}
composed with the Bockstein homomorphism respectively (see
[
GKR
, Remark 16.2]
).

We write
xy
for the multiplication
x\bullet y
.
Now we explicitly define the multiplication map and Browder bracket on
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
and list some of the properties of the Browder bracket because we need these facts
to construct a chain-level stabilization map for the configuration space of a closed manifold (see
Proposition

5.22
and
Lemma

6.11
).

Definition 2.1

(The multiplication map and the Browder bracket)
.

Recall that
E_{n}(2)
is the space of embeddings
f\colon D^{n}\sqcup D^{n}\to D^{n}
such that the restriction of
f
to each component of
D^{n}\sqcup D^{n}
is obtained by compositions of dilations and translations. Such an embedding induces a map on configuration spaces
\text{Conf}(D^{n})\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})\to\text{Conf}(D^{n})
. Therefore, we have a map

\theta\colon E_{n}(2)\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})\times\text{Conf}(D^{n})\to\text{Conf}(D^{n}).

Since
E_{n}(2)
is homotopy equivalent to
S^{n-1}
, by applying the Eilenberg–Zilber map, the map
\theta
induces a map on chain complexes

\theta_{*}\colon C_{*}(S^{n-1};R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R).

For
n\geq 2
, there are generators
\sigma_{0}\in H_{0}(S^{n-1};R)
and
\sigma_{n-1}\in H_{n-1}(S^{n-1};R)
, which come from choosing a point of
S^{n-1}
and an orientation of
S^{n-1}
respectively.
The
multiplication map

x\bullet y
of
x
and
y
is the chain

x\bullet y\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\theta_{*}(\sigma_{0}\otimes x\otimes y).

The
Browder bracket

\left[x,y\right]
of
x
and
y
is the chain

\left[x,y\right]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(-1)^{\text{deg}(x)(n-1)+1}\theta_{*}(\sigma_{n-1}\otimes x\otimes y).

Remark 2.2
.

The multiplication map
x\bullet y
and the Browder bracket
\left[x,y\right]
of
x
and
y
in
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)
have the following geometric descriptions: the multiplication of
x
and
y
is the chain obtained by inserting
x
and
y
in a disk and the Browder bracket of
x
and
y
is the chain obtained by rotating
y
around
x
about
S^{n-1}
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 5.
The multiplication map
x\bullet y
and the Browder bracket
\left[x,y\right]
on
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)

At the level of homology, the Browder bracket has the following properties (see
[
CLM76
, p. 215–218]
and
[
GKR
, p. 176–181]
).

(1)

The Browder bracket is linear in both entries.

(2)

The Browder bracket is symmetric up to a sign:

\left[x,y\right]=(-1)^{\text{1+deg}(x)\text{deg}(y)+(n-1)(\text{deg}(x)+\text{deg}(y)+1)}\left[y,x\right].

(3)

The Browder bracket satisfies the Jacobi identity up to sign:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\text{deg}(x)+n-1)(\text{deg}(z)+n-1)}\left[x,\left[y,z\right]\right]+(-1)^{(\text{deg}(y)+n-1)(\text{deg}(x)+n-1)}\left[y,\left[z,x\right]\right]

\displaystyle+(-1)^{(\text{deg}(z)+n-1)(\text{deg}(y)+n-1)}\left[z,\left[x,y\right]\right].

The Browder bracket satisfies
\left[x,x\right]=0
for homology with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
coefficients
and it also satisfies
\left[x,\left[x,x\right]\right]=0
.

(4)

The Browder bracket is a derivation with respect to multiplication:

\left[x,yz\right]=\left[x,y\right]z+(-1)^{\text{deg}(y)(\text{deg}(x)+n-1)}y\left[x,z\right].

(5)

The Browder bracket with the unit vanishes:
\left[1,x\right]=0
if
1\in H_{0}(A_{0};R)
is the identity element.

(6)

The Browder bracket of a Dyer-Lashof operation vanishes:
\left[x,Q^{s}y\right]=0
.

(7)

\beta\left[x,y\right]=\left[\beta x,y\right]+(-1)^{n-1+\text{deg}(x)}\left[x,\beta y\right]
.

(8)

Let
\text{ad}(x)(y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left[x,y\right]
and for
i>1
, let
\text{ad}^{i}(x)(y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{ad}(x)\big(\text{ad}^{i-1}(x)(y)\big)
. The homology operation
\zeta(-)
can be defined as the map
\beta\xi(-)-\text{ad}^{p-1}(-)(\beta(-))
. We have that

\left[x,\zeta y\right]=0\text{ and }\left[x,\xi y\right]=\text{ad}^{p}(y)(x).

When
p=2
, we have that
\left[x,\xi y\right]=\left[y,\left[y,x\right]\right].

From these operations, Cohen–Lada–May calculated
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
. Before introducing this calculation, we now describe a generalization of
\text{Conf}(M)
and a functor which assigns to a based space
(X,*)
an
E_{n}
-algebra
\mathbf{E_{n}}(X)
because we will need them to study
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty));R)
for
R=\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{Q}
in
Lemma

5.4
.

Definition 2.3
.

We use the notation
\theta
to denote the composition map
\theta\colon E_{n}(k)\times E_{n}(j_{1})\times\cdots\times E_{n}(j_{k})\to E_{n}(j)
, with
j=\sum_{i=1}^{k}j_{i}
, of the operad
E_{n}
and we use the notation
*
to denote the point
E_{n}(0)
(recall that
E_{n}(0)
consists of the unique “embedding” of the empty set into
D^{n}
).
Let
\text{Top}_{*}
denote the category of based topological spaces. Let
\mathbf{E_{n}}\colon\text{Top}_{*}\to\text{Top}_{*}
be the functor defined by sending
(X,*)
to

\mathbf{E_{n}}(X)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigsqcup_{k=0}^{\infty}E_{n}(k)\times_{\Sigma_{k}}X^{k}/\sim,

where for
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in X^{k}
and
c\in E_{n}(k)
,

(\theta(c,(\text{id}_{D^{n}}^{i},*,\text{id}_{D^{n}}^{k-i-1})),(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}))\sim(c,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},*,x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{k-1})).

The space
\mathbf{E_{n}}(X)
can be thought of as a model for the following generalization of
\text{Conf}(M)
when
M
is
D^{n}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
M
be a space and let
(X,x^{\prime})
be a based space.
The
configuration space of M with labels in X
, denoted by
\text{Conf}(M;X)
, is
the quotient space

\bigg(\coprod_{k\geq 0}\big(\{(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k})\in M^{k}:m_{i}\neq m_{j}\text{ if }i\neq j\}\times X^{k}\big)\bigg)/\sim,

where
[(m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}),(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})]
is identified
[(m_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,m_{\sigma(k)}),(x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(k)})]
for
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
and it is also identified with
[(m_{1},\ldots,\hat{m}_{i},\ldots,m_{k}),(x_{1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{i},\ldots,x_{k})]
if
x_{i}=x^{\prime}
.

Roughly speaking, a point
m_{i}x_{i}
vanishes if
x_{i}
converges to the basepoint
x^{\prime}
of
X
. A labelled configuration space can be thought of as a generalization of
\text{Conf}(M)
since
\text{Conf}(M;S^{0})=\text{Conf}(M)
.
The map
E_{n}(k)/\Sigma_{k}\to\text{Conf}_{k}(D^{n})
that records where an embedding
f\colon\sqcup_{i=0}^{k}D^{n}_{i}\to D^{n}
sends the center of each disk
D^{n}_{i}
induces a homotopy equivalence
(E_{n}(k)\times_{\Sigma_{k}}X^{k}/\sim)\to\text{Conf}_{k}(D^{n};X)
and so
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{n}}(S^{0})
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
.

[
CLM76
, p. 225, 227]
fully calculate
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(X);R)
for
R=\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{Q}
, which we now summarize.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
V
be a vector space.
An element
v\in V
is a
Lie word of weight 1
.
Assume that Lie words of weight
i
have been defined for
i<j
. Then a
Lie word of weight j
is any
\left[a,b\right]
, where
a
and
b
are Lie words such that
\text{weight}(a)+\text{weight}(b)=j
.

Instead of giving a full description of
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(X);R)
for
R=\mathbb{F}_{p},\mathbb{Q}
, the following summary will suffice for our purposes. We will give a more precise description of
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)=H_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)
when needed–see
Theorem

2.8
and
Theorem

3.15
.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
CLM76
, p. 225–227]
,
[
GKR
, Theorem 16.4]
)
.

Let
(X,*)
be a based space and suppose that
R
is either
\mathbb{F}_{p}
or
\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
L
denote the set of Lie words of
\tilde{H}_{*}(X;R)
.

(1)

Suppose that
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Let
S
be the set of formal symbols constructed by iterated formal applications of
Q^{s},\beta Q^{s},\xi,\beta\xi,
and
\zeta
to elements of
L
. We also allow zero applications of these operations to
L
. There is a subset
G_{n}\subset S
such that
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(X);\mathbb{F}_{p})
is isomorphic as a ring to the free graded commutative algebra on
G_{n}
.

(2)

Suppose that
R=\mathbb{Q}
. Then
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(X);\mathbb{Q})
is isomorphic to free commutative algebra on
L
modulo the relations in Properties
1
-
5
of the Browder bracket.

We will need the following lemma concerning the set
L
in
Theorem

2.6
when
X=S^{0}
–see
Lemma

5.4
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose that
X
is
S^{0}
,
R
is either
\mathbb{F}_{p}
or
\mathbb{Q}
, and
e\in\tilde{H}_{0}(S^{0};R)
is the class of a point. Then we can take the set
L
in
Theorem

2.6
to be
\{e,\left[e,e\right]\}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

2.6
, elements in
L
are homology classes in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
. It suffices to show that if
w\in L
is a Lie word of weight
j>2
, then
w=0
in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
. In
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
, the only Lie word of weight
3
is
\left[e,\left[e,e\right]\right]
(since the Browder bracket is symmetric up to a choice of sign by Property
2
) and this class is zero (again by Property
2
). Up to sign, the only Lie words of weight
4
are of the form
\left[e,\left[e,\left[e,e\right]\right]\right]
and
\left[\left[e,e\right],\left[e,e\right]\right]
. We have that
\left[e,\left[e,\left[e,e\right]\right]\right]
is zero in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
, since
\left[e,\left[e,e\right]\right]=0
. By the Jacobi identity of the Browder bracket (Property
3
),

0=\left[\left[e,e\right],\left[e,e\right]\right]+\left[e,\left[e,\left[e,e\right]\right]\right]+\left[e,\left[\left[e,e\right],e\right]\right].

Since
\left[e,\left[\left[e,e\right],e\right]\right]=0
in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
, we have that
\left[\left[e,e\right],\left[e,e\right]\right]
is zero as well. Suppose by induction that for
j\geq 5
and for any Lie word
v
with
3\leq\text{weight}(v)\leq j
,
we have that
v=0
in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
. If
w\in L
is a Lie word of weight
j+1
, then by definition
w=\left[a,b\right]
, where
\text{weight}(a)+\text{weight}(a)=j+1
. Since
j\geq 5
, we have that either
3\leq\text{weight}(a)\leq j
or
3\leq\text{weight}(b)\leq j
. Since the set of all Lie words of weight
3,\ldots,j
are zero in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
, either
a
or
b
is zero in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0});R)
, and so
w
is as well.
∎

From
Theorem

2.6
, Cohen–Lada–May calculated
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
.
When
n>2
,
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
has a somewhat complicated description which we omit because we will only need some properties of
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
later on in
Section

3.3
(see
Theorem

3.15
). We have the following explicit computation of
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
CLM76
, p. 347–348]
)
.

Let
e\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
denote the class of a point.

(1)

For
j>0
, let
(a,b)=(2^{j}-1,2^{j})
and let
x_{j}
denote the class
\xi\cdots\xi e\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{2})
, where
\xi
is applied
j
times. We have that

H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}\left[e,x_{1},\ldots\right].

(2)

Let
p
be an odd prime.
Let
z_{0}\in H_{1}(\text{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
denote the class
\left[e,e\right]
. For
j>0
, let
(c,d)=(2p^{j}-1,2p^{j})
and let
y_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\beta\xi\cdots\xi z_{0}\in H_{c-1}(\text{Conf}_{d}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
and let
z_{j}
denote the class
\xi\cdots\xi z_{0}\in H_{c}(\text{Conf}_{d}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
, where
\xi
is applied
j
times.
Let
\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\left[z_{0},z_{1},\ldots\right]
denote the exterior algebra generated by the classes
z_{j}
. We have that

H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}\left[e,y_{1},\ldots\right]\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\left[z_{0},z_{1},\ldots\right].

Definition 2.9
.

Given a class
x\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
,
let
\text{deg}(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}a
denote the homological degree of
x
and let
\text{par}(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}b
denote the number of particles of
x
. We introduce this notation to keep track of how homology operations interact with the homological degree and the number of particles of a homology class. Fix a representative cycle
x^{\prime}\in C_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
of
x
and define

\displaystyle t_{x}\colon C_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)

\displaystyle\to C_{i+\text{deg}(x)}(\text{Conf}_{k+\text{par}(x)}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\omega x^{\prime}

to be the “multiplication by
x
” map.
By abuse of notation, let
t_{x}
also denote the induced map on homology.

Now we fix some notation for an iterated mapping cone to formulate higher-order homological stability.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
t_{i}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k+k_{i}}(M);R)
for
i=1,\ldots,m
be chain maps that all commute with each other.
Recursively define the
m-th iterated mapping cone

\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{i}))
associated to the
m
-tuple
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m})
as follows:
set
\text{Cone}_{k}^{1}(M,(t_{i}))
to be the mapping cone

\text{Cone}_{k}^{1}(M,(t_{i}))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{Cone}(t_{1}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k-k_{1}}(M);R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)),

and for
m>1
, set
\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{i}))
to be the induced mapping cone

\text{Cone }\big(t_{m}\colon\text{Cone}_{k-k_{m}}^{m-1}(M,(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m-1}))\to\text{Cone}_{k}^{m-1}(M,(t_{1},\ldots,t_{m-1}))\big).

Let
\text{Cone}^{m}(M,(t_{i}))
denote
\bigsqcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{i}))
.

Suppose that
M
is an open connected manifold of dimension
n
and that we have an embedding

\iota\colon M\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M
as in
Lemma

3.1
.
Given
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{m}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
, we describe how to construct stabilization maps
t_{\omega_{j}}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)
for
j=1,\ldots,m
that
commute with each other.
For simplicity, we describe the construction when
m=2
(the construction of the mapping cone is similar when
m>2
).

Fix an embedding
\iota\colon M\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to M
as in
Lemma

3.1
. Let
\iota_{j}\colon M\sqcup\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}\to M
denote the restriction of
\iota
to
M\sqcup D^{n}_{j}
and let
\text{Conf}(\iota_{j})\colon\text{Conf}(M)\times\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j})\to\text{Conf}(M)
denote the induced map on configuration spaces by applying
\iota_{j}
pointwise. The construction of
\iota
(by a generalization of the proof of
[
KM15
, Lemma 2.4]
) comes from picking two disjoint rays
\rho_{j}\cong[0,\infty)\subset M
for
j=1,2
, taking a tubular neighborhood
N_{j}
of
\rho_{j}
such that
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are disjoint, and inserting
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j}
inside
N_{j}
. Outside of
N_{1}\cup N_{2}
, the map
\iota
is the identity. As a result, by picking the tubular neighborhoods
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
to be disjoint, we ensure that
the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\sqcup\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\sqcup\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\sqcup\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}

Therefore, we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(M)\times\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n})\times\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(M)\times\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(M)\times\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{1})}

From the map
\text{Conf}(\iota_{j})
and applying the Eilenberg–Zilber map,
we have an induced map on chain complexes

\text{Conf}(\iota_{j})_{*}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{j});R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R).

As a result, we have another commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n});R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n});R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{2}^{n});R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n});R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{2})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{2})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\iota_{1})_{*}}

Pick a representative cycle
\omega_{j}^{\prime}\in C_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
for the class
\omega_{j}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
and set
t_{\omega_{j}}
to be the restriction

t_{\omega_{j}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{Conf}(\iota_{j})_{*}(-\otimes\omega_{j}^{\prime})\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R).

From the previous commutative diagram, we have that

t_{\omega_{1}}t_{\omega_{2}}=t_{\omega_{2}}t_{\omega_{1}}
.

Now we obtain stable ranges for homological and secondary homological stability for
\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
e\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
be the class of a point, let
x_{j}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
y_{j}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
denote the homology classes from
Theorem

2.8
.
Let
m
a positive integer and define
D(p,m,k)

to be the constant

D(p,m,k)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\begin{cases}(2^{m}-1)k/2^{m}&\text{if p is }2\\
\displaystyle\frac{(p^{m}-1)k-\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}p^{m}-2p^{j}}{p^{m}}&\text{if p is odd.}\\
\end{cases}

Let
\omega_{0}
be the class
e
and for
m>1
, let
w_{m-1}
be the class
x_{m-1}
if
p
is
2
and let
w_{m-1}
be the class
y_{m-1}
if
p
is odd. Let
x
be a homology class in
H_{i+\text{deg}(w_{m-1})}(\text{Conf}_{k+\text{par}(w_{m-1})}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
and suppose that
i\leq D(p,m,k)
.

(1)

If
m
is
1
, then
x
is in the ideal
(e)
.

(2)

If
m
is greater than
1
and
p
is
2
, then the class
x
is in the ideal
I_{m-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(e,x_{1},\ldots,x_{m-1})
.

(3)

If
m
is greater than
1
and
p
is odd, then the class
x
is in the ideal
J_{m-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(e,y_{1},\ldots,y_{m-1})
.

Proof.

We first consider Part
3
. Suppose that a monomial
x\in H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
p>2
, is in the complement of the ideal
I_{m-1}
. Then
x
is a product of elements in the set
R_{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{y_{a},z_{b}:a\geq m,b\geq 0\}
. We want to show that for such an
x
we have that
i-\text{deg}(y_{m-1})>D(p,m,k-\text{par}(y_{m-1}))
. We have that
i-\text{deg}(y_{m-1})=i-(2p^{m-1}-2)
and

D(p,m,k-\text{par}(y_{m-1}))=\displaystyle\frac{(p^{m}-1)(k-2p^{m-1})-\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}p^{m}-2p^{j}}{p^{m}}.

Note that

-\text{deg}(y_{m-1})>-(p^{m}-1)\text{par}(y_{m-1})/p^{m}

since
\text{deg}(y_{m-1})p^{m}=2(p^{m-1}-1)p^{m}
while
(p^{m}-1)\text{par}(y_{m-1})=2(p^{m}-1)p^{m}
. Therefore, to prove Part
3
, it suffices to show that for any
x\in H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
that is a product of elements in
R_{m}
, we have that
i\geq D(p,m,k)
. Similarly, to prove Part
2
, it suffices to show that for any
x\in H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
that is a product of elements in the set
\{x_{n}:n\geq m\}
, we have that
i\geq D(2,m,k)
. We say that a class
x\in H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is in the
m-th unstable range
if

i\geq D(p,m,k)
.

We first deal with the case
p>2
. Let
A(p,m)=p^{m}
,
B(p,m)=p^{m}-1
and let
C(p,m)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}p^{m}-2p^{j}
so that
D(p,m,k)=\frac{B(p,m)k-C(p,m)}{A(p,m)}
.
For all
n\geq m
, let
\alpha_{n}
denote
y_{n}
or
z_{n}
. Since
\text{par}(\alpha_{n})=2p^{n}
and
\text{deg}(\alpha_{n})\geq 2p^{n}-2
, we have that
\frac{\text{deg}(\alpha_{n})}{\text{par}(\alpha_{n})}\geq\frac{p^{n}-1}{p^{n}}\geq\frac{B(p,m)}{A(p,m)}=\frac{p^{m}-1}{p^{m}}
and
\alpha_{n}
is in the m-th unstable range. Since
\text{par}(xy)=\text{par}(x)+\text{par}(y)
(likewise for
deg
), it follows that for every
x
that is a product of elements in the set
\{y_{n},z_{n}:n\geq m\}\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}S_{m}\subset R_{m}
,
we have that
A(p,m)\text{deg}(x)\geq B(p,m)\text{par}(x)
. We now deal with elements
y
in
R_{m}\setminus S_{m}
.
Write
y
as
y=z_{0}^{\epsilon_{0}}\cdots z_{m-1}^{\epsilon_{m-1}}z
, with
\epsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}
and
z\in S_{m}
.

To show that
y
is in the m-th unstable range, it suffices to prove that
B(p,m)\text{par}(y)-A(p,m)\text{deg}(y)\leq C(p,m)
. Note that

B(p,m)\text{par}(y)-A(p,m)\text{deg}(y)

=B(p,m)\big(\text{par}(z)+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\text{par}(z_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}})\big)-A(p,m)\big(\text{deg}(z)+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\text{deg}(z_{j}^{\epsilon_{j}})\big)

=B(p,m)\big(\text{par}(z)+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\text{par}(z_{j})\big)-A(p,m)\big(\text{deg}(z)+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\text{deg}(z_{j})\big)
.

Since
z
is in
S_{m}
, we have that
B(p,m)\text{par}(z)-A(p,m)\text{deg}(z)\leq 0
. We now check that

B(p,m)\big(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\text{par}(z_{j})\big)-A(p,m)\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\text{deg}(z_{j})\leq C(p,m)
.

Note that

B(p,m)\big(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\text{par}(z_{j})\big)-A(p,m)\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\text{deg}(z_{j})

=(p^{m}-1)(\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}2p^{j})-p^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}(2p^{j}-1)

=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\big(2p^{j}(p^{m}-1)-(2p^{j}-1)p^{m}\big)

=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}\big(2p^{j}(p^{m}-p^{m}-1)+p^{m}\big)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}(p^{m}-2p^{j})

Since
p>2
and
m>j
,
p^{m}-2p^{j}>0
. In addition, the sum
\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\epsilon_{j}(p^{m}-2p^{j})
achieves its maximum precisely when
\epsilon_{j}=1
for all
j
, and in this case this sum equals
C(p,m)
.

Now we deal with the case
p=2
. Note that
\text{par}(x_{n})=2^{n}
and
\text{deg}(x_{n})=2^{n}-1
. For all
n\geq m
, we have that
\frac{\text{deg}(x_{n})}{\text{par}(x_{n})}\geq\frac{2^{m}-1}{2^{m}}
, so
x_{n}
is in the m-th unstable range. By the same argument as the case
p>2
, it follows that if
x
is a product of elements in the set
\{x_{n}:n\geq m\}
, then
x
is in the m-th unstable range.
∎

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
e\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
be the class of a point, let
x_{j}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
y_{j}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
denote the homology classes from
Theorem

2.8
.
Let
D(p,m,k)
denote the constant from
Proposition

2.11
.

(1)

The map

t_{e}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for

i\leq D(p,1,k)
.

(2)

Suppose that
p
is
2
.
Let
\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{x_{j}}))
denote the m-th iterated mapping cone associated to the collection
(t_{e},t_{x_{1}},t_{x_{2}},\ldots,t_{x_{m-1}})
as defined in
Definition

2.10
. The map

t_{x_{m}}\colon H_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{x_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H_{i+2^{m}-1}(\text{Cone}_{k+2^{m}}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{x_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{2})

is an isomorphism
for
i\leq D(2,m+1,k)
.

(3)

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime.
Let
\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}))
denote the m-th iterated mapping cone associated to the collection
(t_{e},t_{y_{1}},t_{y_{2}},\ldots,t_{y_{m-1}})
. The map on homology groups of mapping cones

t_{y_{m}}\colon H_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{i+2p^{m}-2}(\text{Cone}_{k+2p^{m}}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism
for
i\leq D(p,m+1,k)
.

Proof.

We prove this result for the case
p
is odd and
m
is greater than
1
since the proofs for the other cases are similar.

To show that the map

t_{y_{m}}\colon C_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\to C_{i+2p^{m}-2}(\text{Cone}_{k+2p^{m}}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})

induces an isomorphism on homology in a range, we will show that the map
t_{y_{m}}
induces an injection on homology and that the homology of the
m+1
iterated mapping cone

H_{i+2p^{m}-2}(\text{Cone}_{k+2p^{m}}^{m+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e},t_{y_{1}},\ldots,t_{y_{m}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})

vanishes in the same range. Using induction on
m
, we first show that the map

t_{y_{m-1}}\colon H_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{i+2p^{m}-2}(\text{Cone}_{k+2p^{m}}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})

is injective and that
H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e},t_{y_{1}},\ldots,t_{y_{m-1}})))
is isomorphic to
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})/(e,y_{1},\ldots,y_{m-1})
.

Suppose that
m=1
. The map
t_{y_{0}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{e}\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is injective. We have a long exact sequence of homology groups

\cdots\rightarrow H_{*-1}\big(\text{Cone}_{k}^{1}(R^{2},(t_{e}))\big)\xrightarrow{\partial_{*}}H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\xrightarrow{t_{e}}H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\rightarrow H_{*}\big(\text{Cone}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e}))\big)\rightarrow\cdots.

Since the map
t_{e}\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
is injective, the boundary homomorphism

\partial_{*}\colon H_{*-1}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{1}(R^{2},(t_{e}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})

is the zero map and we have a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\xrightarrow{t_{e}}H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\rightarrow H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\rightarrow 0.

As a result, we have that
H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e}));\mathbb{F}_{p})
is isomorphic to
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})/(e)
.

Suppose now that
m>1
and that for
l=1,\ldots,m-1
,
H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{l}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e},t_{y_{1}},\ldots,t_{y_{l-1}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})
is isomorphic to
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})/(e,y_{1},\ldots,y_{l-1})
. Since

H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})/(e,y_{1},\ldots,y_{m-2})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}\left[y_{m-1},y_{m},\ldots\right]\otimes\Lambda_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\left[z_{0},z_{1},\ldots\right],

the map

t_{y_{m-1}}\colon H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e},t_{y_{1}},\ldots,t_{y_{m-2}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e},t_{y_{1}},\ldots,t_{y_{m-2}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})

is injective. From the same argument for the case
m=1
, we have a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\xrightarrow{t_{y_{m-1}}}H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\rightarrow H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\rightarrow 0

and so
H_{*}(\text{Cone}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})
is isomorphic to
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})/(e,y_{1},\ldots,y_{m-1})
.

We now show that
H_{i+2p^{m}-2}(\text{Cone}_{k+2p^{m}}^{m+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2},(t_{e},t_{y_{1}},\ldots,t_{y_{m}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})
vanishes for
i\leq D(p,m+1,k)
.
This is equivalent to showing that if
x
is a class in
H_{i+2p^{m}-2}(\text{Conf}_{k+2p^{m}}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
with
i\leq D(p,m+1,k)
, then
x
is in the ideal
(e,y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
, which follows immediately from
Proposition

2.11
.
∎

3.
Stability for Configuration Spaces of an Open Manifold

In this section, we prove secondary homological stability for the unordered configuration space of an open connected manifold
M
. Our proof mostly follows from an argument from
[
KM15
, Section 4]
. In
[
KM15
, Section 4]
, Kupers–Miller streamline the argument behind the proof of
[
Seg79
, Proposition A.1]
in order to prove homological stability for the unordered configurations space of an open connected manifold of dimension greater than 2.
In
Section

3.1
, we review the setup from
[
KM15
]
. In
Section

3.2
, we prove secondary homological stability when the dimension of
M
is
2
. In
Section

3.3
, we prove secondary homological stability when the dimension of
M
is greater than
2
.
For simplicity, we always assume that the dimension of
M
is even (or equal to one) and orientable–these conditions are equivalent to
\text{Conf}(M)
being orientable. If
M
is odd dimensional or not orientable, secondary homological stability still holds by an argument at the end of Appendix A of
[
Seg79
, p. 71]
.

3.1.
Homological Stability for Configuration Spaces of an Open Manifold

Stability for the configuration space of an open manifold depends crucially on the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
KM15
, Lemma 2.4]
)
.

Let
M
be an open connected manifold of dimension
n
. For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an embedding
\iota\colon M\bigsqcup(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{R}^{n})\to M
such that
\iota|_{M}
is isotopic to
\text{id}_{M}
.

Furthermore, the embedding
\iota
can be chosen so that
M
has an exhaustion by the interiors of compact manifolds
\bar{M}_{j}
admitting a finite handle decomposition with a single
0
-handle and
\iota
restricts to an embedding
\iota_{j}\colon M_{j}\bigsqcup(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{R}^{n})\to M_{j}
such that
\iota_{j}|_{M_{j}}
is isotopic to
\text{id}_{M_{j}}
.

Kupers–Miller only considered the case
m=1
, but the general case follows immediately since an embedding
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
exists. An embedding
\iota\colon\bigsqcup M_{j}\to M
and a collection of nonnegative integers
k_{j}
with
\sum k_{j}=k
induce a map of configuration spaces

\text{Conf}(\iota)\colon\prod\text{Conf}_{k_{j}}(M_{j})\to\text{Conf}_{k}(M)

by applying the embedding
\iota
to each point of the configuration.

Definition 3.2
.

Suppose that
M
is an open manifold. Fix an embedding
\iota\colon M\sqcup\mathbb{R}^{n}\to M
as in
Lemma

3.1
and define

\text{Conf}(\iota)\colon\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\times\text{Conf}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\text{Conf}_{k+l}(M)

to be the induced map on configuration spaces. Let

\text{Conf}(\iota)_{*}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)

be the composition of the Eilenberg–Zilber map

EZ\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\otimes C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\times\text{Conf}_{l}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)

with the map on chain complexes induced from
\text{Conf}(\iota)
.

Given a class
z\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
, fix a representative cycle
z^{\prime}\in C_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
of
z
and define

t_{z}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to C_{*+a}(\text{Conf}_{k+b}(M);R)

to be the map sending
\omega\in C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
to
\text{Conf}(\iota)_{*}(\omega\otimes z^{\prime})
.
Let
t_{z}
also denote the induced maps on (co)homology (it will be clear from context which map we mean).

Remark 3.3
.

Our reason for having that the restriction
\iota|_{M}
is isotopic to
\text{id}_{M}
is the following:
if
z\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
is the class of the empty configuration, then the map
t_{z}\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
is the identity map instead of a non-identity automorphism.

Definition 3.4
.

If
e\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
is the class of a point, the map

t_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{e}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)

is called the
stabilization map
.

McDuff showed that the induced map on integral homology
t_{1}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{Z})\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{Z})
is an isomorphism for all
i\ll k
if
M
is a smooth manifold of finite type (
[
McD75
, p. 104]
). Isomorphisms of the form
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\cong H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)
for all
i\ll k
are called
homological stability
. To prove homological stability for
\text{Conf}_{k}(M)
, it helps to work with compactly supported cohomology. When
X
is a locally compact Hausdorff space, the reduced cohomology
\tilde{H}^{*}(X^{+},X^{+}\setminus X;R)
of the point-compactification
X^{+}
of
X
is isomorphic to
H^{*}_{c}(X;R)
. One advantage of working with compactly supported cohomology is the following well-known long exact sequence.

Proposition 3.5

(e.g.
[
Ive86
]
, III.7.6)
.

Let
R
be an abelian group, W be a locally compact and locally path-connected Hausdorff space, and
A\subset W
a closed subspace that is also locally path-connected. Let
U=W\setminus A
denote its complement. There is a long exact sequence in compactly supported cohomology

\cdots\to H^{*}_{c}(U;R)\to H^{*}_{c}(W;R)\to H^{*}_{c}(A;R)\to H^{*+1}_{c}(U;R)\to\cdots

The group
R
can also be replaced by a twisted coefficient system on
W
.

The maps
H^{*}_{c}(U;R)\to H^{*}_{c}(W;R)
and
H^{*}_{c}(W;R)\to H^{*}_{c}(A;R)
in
Proposition

3.5
come from the extension by zero map
C^{*}_{c}(U;R)\to C^{*}_{c}(W;R)
and restriction map
C^{*}_{c}(W;R)\to C^{*}_{c}(A;R)
respectively.
For
i=1,2
, let
W_{i}
and
A_{i}
satisfy the hypothesis of
W
and
A
, respectively, in
Proposition

3.5
and let
U_{i}=W_{i}\setminus A_{i}
.
From the functoriality of extension by zero and restriction, given a map
C^{*}_{c}(W_{1};R)\to C^{*}_{c}(W_{2};R)
that restricts to maps
C^{*}_{c}(A_{1};R)\to C^{*}_{c}(A_{2};R)
and
C^{*}_{c}(U_{1};R)\to C^{*}_{c}(U_{2};R)
, we have an induced map of long exact sequences in compactly supported cohomology
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{c}(U_{1};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{c}(W_{1};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{c}(A_{1};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*+1}_{c}(U_{1};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{c}(U_{2};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{c}(W_{2};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{c}(A_{2};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*+1}_{c}(U_{2};R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}

We now construct stabilization maps on
C_{c}^{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)
.
Given
z\in H^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{l}(D^{n});R)
, fix a representative compactly suppported cocycle
z^{\prime}\in C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{l}(D^{n});R)
to get a restriction

K(-\otimes z^{\prime})
of the Künneth map

K\colon C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\otimes C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{l}(D^{n});R)\to C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\times\text{Conf}_{l}(D^{n});R).

The map

\text{Conf}(\iota)\colon\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\times\text{Conf}_{l}(D^{n})\to\text{Conf}_{k+l}(M)
is an open embedding for all nonnegative
k
and
l
. Therefore, we have an induced map

\text{Conf}(\iota)^{*}_{c}\colon C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\times\text{Conf}_{l}(D^{n});R)\to C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k+l}(M);R)

via extension by zero. Define

t_{z}\colon C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k+l}(M);R)

to be the composition

\text{Conf}(\iota)^{*}_{c}\circ K(-\otimes z^{\prime})
.

Specializing to the case
l=1
, when
z\in H^{n}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{1}(D^{n});R)\cong H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(D^{n});R)
corresponds to the class of a point (under Poincaré duality), we get a map
t_{1}\colon H_{c}^{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to H_{c}^{*+n}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)
.
By our assumption that
\text{Conf}(M)
is orientable and Poincaré duality, the map
t_{1}
being an isomorphism in a range is equivalent to the stabilization map
t_{1}\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)
being an isomorphism in the dual range. In particular, one can show:

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
M
be an open connected surface and let
D(p,m,k)
denote the constant from
Proposition

2.11
.
The stabilization map

t_{1}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})

from
Definition

3.4
is an isomorphism for
i<D(p,1,k)
and a surjection for
i=D(p,1,k)
.
As a consequence, the map
t_{1}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{Z}\left[1/2\right])\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{Z}\left[1/2\right])
is an isomorphism for
3i<2k-1
and a surjection for
3i=2k-1.
These ranges for homological stability are optimal.

Proof.

The proposition follows by applying the argument of
[
KM15
, Sections 3 and 4]
and using Part
1
of
Corollary

2.12
.
∎

3.2.
Secondary Homological Stability for Configuration Spaces of an Open Surface

We now describe secondary homological stability for
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})
when
M
is an open connected surface. For simplicity, we assume that
p
is an odd prime number (the case
p
is
2
is similar).

Let
y_{1}\in H_{2p-2}(\text{Conf}_{2p}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
denote the class defined in
Theorem

2.8
and consider the map

t_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{\displaystyle y_{1}}\colon C_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to C_{i+2p-2}(\text{Conf}_{k+2p}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})

(if
p
were
2
, we would consider the map
t_{\displaystyle x_{1}}
corresponding to
x_{1}\in H_{1}(\text{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{2})
defined in
Theorem

2.8
). The map
t_{2}
does not induce an isomorphism of homology groups. But, we can consider
the relative chain complex

C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{Cone}\big(t_{1}\colon C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M))\to C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M))\big)=C_{*}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{1}(M,(t_{1}));\mathbb{F}_{p}).

Since the maps
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
can be constructed to commute with each other (see the discussion following
Definition

2.10
),
we have a
map of relative chain complexes

t_{2}\colon C_{i}\big(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p}\big)\to C_{i+2p-2}\big(\text{Conf}_{k+2p}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+2p-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p}\big)

This map of relative chain complexes is called the
secondary stabilization map
.
Similar to the isomorphism

t_{y_{1}}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{i+2p-2}(\text{Conf}_{k+2p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\text{Conf}_{k+2p-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})

from
Corollary

2.12
, the secondary stabilization map
t_{2}
induces an isomorphism of relative homology groups. This phenomenon is called
secondary homological stability
.

To prove secondary homological stability, we pass to compactly supported cohomology. Let
y_{1}^{*}\in H^{2p+2}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{2p}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
denote the compactly supported cohomology class corresponding to
y_{1}
under Poincaré duality. We have a map

t_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{y_{1}^{*}}\colon C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to C^{*+2p+2}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k+2p}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})

and a relative cochain complex

C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\text{Cone}\big(t_{1}\colon C^{*-n}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to C^{*}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\big).

The maps
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
can be constructed so that they commute with each other, so we have a secondary stabilization map for compactly supported cochains

t_{2}\colon C^{i}_{c}\big(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p}\big)\to C^{i+2p+2}_{c}\big(\text{Conf}_{k+2p}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+2p-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p}\big).

By Poincaré duality, the following proposition is equivalent to secondary homological stability.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
M
be an open connected surface and let
D(p,m,k)
denote the constant from
Proposition

2.11
.

(1)

Suppose that
p
is
2
. The secondary stabilization map induces an isomorphism

t_{2}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{i+3}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k+2}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})

for
i>2k-D(2,2,k)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(2,2,k)
.

(2)

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime and that
M
is orientable. The secondary stabilization map induces an isomorphism

t_{2}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k+2p}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+2p-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})

for
i>2k-D(p,2,k)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(p,2,k)
.

By the following lemma and
Lemma

3.1
, it suffices to prove this proposition when
M
is the interior of a compact surface admitting a finite handle decomposition with a single 0-handle.

Lemma 3.8

(e.g.
[
KM15
, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Every open connected manifold has an exhaustion by compact manifolds admitting a finite handle decomposition with a single 0-handle.

As a result, we can prove stability for a general connected open manifold
M
from a colimit argument involving an exhaustion of
M
(see
[
KM15
, p. 10]
).
The main reasons for assuming
M
is the interior of such a compact manifold are the following well-known results.

Lemma 3.9

(e.g.
[
KM15
, Lemma 2.2]
)
.

Let

M
be the interior of an
n
-dimensional manifold admitting a finite handle decomposition with a single 0-handle. Then there is closed subspace
X
of
M
homeomorphic to an open subset of a finite complex of dimension at most
n-1
, such that
M\setminus X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Lemma 3.10

(e.g.
[
KM15
, Lemma 4.1]
)
.

Let
R
be a ring and
M
the interior of an
n
-dimensional manifold
\bar{M}
admitting a finite handle decomposition. The homology
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
is a finitely-generated
R
-module for all
i\geq 0
.

To prove
Proposition

3.7
we use a five lemma argument due to
[
Seg79
, Appendix A]
involving
Proposition

3.5
and the following filtration on
\text{Conf}(M)
.

Definition 3.11
.

Suppose that
M
is the interior of an
n
-dimensional manifold admitting a finite handle decomposition with a single 0-handle. Pick a closed subspace
X\subset M
with the properties described in
Lemma

3.9
.
Define
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M)
to be the subspace of
\text{Conf}_{k}(M)
with at least
j
points in
X
.

Note that
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M)
is empty for
j>k
,
\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k}(M)\subset\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M)
, the complement
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M)\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k}(M)
is homeomorphic to
\text{Conf}_{k-j}(M\setminus X)\times\text{Conf}_{j}(X)
, and
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M)
is closed for all
j,k\geq 0
.

Recall that the construction of the secondary stabilization map depends on choosing
an embedding

\iota\colon M\sqcup D^{n}_{1}\sqcup D^{n}_{2}\to M
such that the restriction of
\iota
to
M
is isotopic to the identity. It is implicit in the proofs of
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

3.9
and in the definition of the stabilization map that we can choose
X\subset M
and
\iota
such that
\iota
restricts to an embedding
\iota\colon(M\setminus X)\sqcup D^{n}_{1}\sqcup D^{n}_{2}\to M\setminus X
with the restriction of
\iota
to
M\setminus X
isotopic to the identity. Therefore, the map

\text{Conf}(\iota)\colon\text{Conf}_{k}(M)\times\text{Conf}_{l_{1}}(D^{1})\times\text{Conf}_{l_{2}}(D^{2})\to\text{Conf}_{k+l_{1}+l_{2}}(M)

restricts to a map
\text{Conf}(\iota)\colon\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M)\times\text{Conf}_{l_{1}}(D^{1})\times\text{Conf}_{l_{2}}(D^{2})\to\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+l_{1}+l_{2}}(M)
for all
j
. As a result, by restriction we have maps
t_{1}\colon C^{i-n}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}(M);R)\to C^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M);R)
and
t_{2}\colon C^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M);R)\to C^{i+3}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2}(M);R)

which commute. So we have an induced map

t_{2}\colon C^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M),\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}(M);R)\to C^{i+3}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2}(M),\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+1}(M);R).

Proposition

3.7
is a consequence of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
M
be the interior of a connected surface admitting a finite handle decomposition with a single
0
-handle. Pick
X\subset M
as in
Lemma

3.9
.
Let
D(p,m,k)
denote the constant from
Proposition

2.11
.

(1)

Suppose that
p
is
2
. The map

t_{2}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M),\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H^{i+3}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2}(M),\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})

is an isomorphism for

i>2k-D(2,2,k)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(2,2,k)
.

(2)

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime and that
M
is orientable. The map

t_{2}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M),\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2p}(M),\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1+2p}(M);\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for

i>2k-D(p,2,k)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(p,2,k)
.

Proof.

We prove this proposition for the case
p
is odd since the proof for the case
p
is
2
is similar. The proof is essentially the same as
[
Seg79
, Propositionn A.1]
and
[
KM15
, Proposition 4.4]
, but with slightly different maps and ranges. Let
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}
denote
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(M)
.

Consider the long exact sequence of
Proposition

3.5
associated to the inclusion of pairs
(\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1})\subset(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1})
coming from the inclusion
\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k}\subset\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}

\cdots\to H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to\cdots.

The restriction of
t_{2}\colon C_{*}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to C_{*+1}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+1},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{p})
to

C_{*}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})=C_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k-j}(M\setminus X)\times\text{Conf}_{j}(X),\text{Conf}_{k-1-j}(M\setminus X)\times\text{Conf}_{j}(X);\mathbb{F}_{p})

is the map
t_{2}\times\text{id}_{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{2}\times(\text{id}_{\text{Conf}_{j}(X)})_{*}
.
From the discussion following
Proposition

3.5
, the secondary stabilization map
t_{2}
induces a map between long exact sequences:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2p}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k+2p},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1+2p}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1+2p};\mathbb{F}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2p},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1+2p};\mathbb{F}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k+2p},\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1+2p};\mathbb{F}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}\times\text{id}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}

We want to show that the middle horizontal map

(*)_{k}

t_{2}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2p},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1+2p};\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for
i>2k-D(p,2,k)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(p,2,k)
.

For
j>k+2p
, the pairs
(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1})
and
(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2p},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1+2p})
are both empty, so

(
∗
)

k

holds for
j>k+2p
.
Proceed by downward induction on
j
and suppose that

(
∗
)

k

holds for
j+1
. Then by our induction hypothesis, the lower horizontal map

t_{2}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k+2p},\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1+2p};\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for
i>2k-D(p,2,k)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(p,2,k)
. To prove that

(
∗
)

k

holds, by the five lemma it suffices to show:

(\dagger)_{k,j}

t_{2}\times\text{id}_{*}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k+2p}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k+2p},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1+2p}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1+2p};\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for
i>2k-D(p,2,k-j)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(p,2,k-j).

Recall that since
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k}=\text{Conf}_{k-j}(M\setminus X)\times\text{Conf}_{j}(X)
and
M\setminus X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the pair
(\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k},\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k-1}\setminus\mathcal{G}^{j+1}_{k-1})
is isomorphic to
\big(\text{Conf}_{k-j}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\text{Conf}_{j}(X),\text{Conf}_{k-1-j}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\text{Conf}_{j}(X)\big)
. By
Corollary

2.12
and Poincaré duality,
the secondary stabilization map

t_{2}\colon H^{i}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k-j}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\text{Conf}_{k-1-j}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i+2p+2}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k-j+2p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\text{Conf}_{k-1-j+2p}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for
i>2k-D(p,2,k-j)
and a surjection for
i=2k-D(p,2,k-j)
.

Since we are working with cohomology with coefficients in a field, the Künneth theorem holds. Suppose that
x\in H^{a}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k-j+2p}(M\setminus X),\text{Conf}_{k-j-1+2p}(M\setminus X);\mathbb{F}_{p})
and
y\in H^{b}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{j}(X),\text{Conf}_{j-1}(X);\mathbb{F}_{p})
,
and that
x\otimes y
is not in the image of
t_{2}\times\text{id}_{*}
. We have that
\text{deg}(x)<2k-D(p,2,k-j)
. Since the dimension of
X
is at most
n-1=1
,
H^{i}_{c}\big(\text{Conf}_{j}(X);\mathbb{F}_{p}\big)=0
for
i>j
. Since
y\neq 0
, we have
b\leq j
. Since
\text{deg}(x\otimes y)=\text{deg}(x)+\text{deg}(y)
, the homological degree of
x\otimes y
satisfies

\displaystyle\text{deg}(x\otimes y)

\displaystyle<\big(2(k-j)-D(p,2,k-j)\big)+j

\displaystyle=2(k-j)-\frac{(p^{2}-1)(k-j)-(2p^{2}-2p-2)}{p^{2}}+j

\displaystyle=(\frac{p^{2}+1}{p^{2}})k+\frac{2p^{2}-2p-2}{p^{2}}-\frac{j}{p^{2}}

\displaystyle<(\frac{p^{2}+1}{p^{2}})k+\frac{2p^{2}-2p-2}{p^{2}}

\displaystyle=2k-\frac{(p^{2}-1)k-(2p^{2}-2p-2)}{p^{2}}=2k-D(p,2,k).

Therefore,
t_{2}\times\text{id}_{*}
is surjective for
i\geq 2k-D(p,2,k)
.
In addition, note that since
M\setminus X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the secondary stabilization map is always injective on
H^{a}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{k-j+2p}(M\setminus X),\text{Conf}_{k-1-j+2p}(M\setminus X);\mathbb{F}_{p})
by
Corollary

2.12
. Since
H^{b}_{c}(\text{Conf}_{j}(X),\text{Conf}_{j-1}(X);\mathbb{F}_{p})
is a flat
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-module, it follows that
t_{2}\times\text{id}_{*}
is injective and so

(
†
)

k
,
j

holds.
∎

We also have the following analogue of
Corollary

2.12
.

Corollary 3.13
.

Suppose that
M
is an open connected surface.
Let
x_{m}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
y_{m}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{p})
denote the homology classes from
Theorem

2.8
.
Let
D(p,m,k)
denote the constant defined in
Proposition

2.11
.

(1)

Suppose that
p
is
2
. Let
\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle x_{j}}))
denote the m-th iterated mapping cone associated to the collection
(t_{1},t_{\displaystyle x_{1}},\ldots,t_{\displaystyle x_{m-1}})
as defined in
Definition

2.10
.
The map

t_{\displaystyle x_{m}}\colon H_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle x_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H_{i+2^{m}-1}(\text{Cone}_{k+2^{m}}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle x_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{2})

is an isomorphism for
i<D(2,m+1,k)
and a surjection for
i=D(2,m+1,k).

(2)

Suppose that
p
is an odd prime and that
M
is orientable. Let
\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{y_{j}}))
denote the m-th iterated mapping cone associated to the collection
