(t_{1},t_{\displaystyle y_{1}},\ldots,t_{\displaystyle y_{m-1}})
.
The map

t_{\displaystyle y_{m}}\colon H_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H_{i+2p^{m}-2}(\text{Cone}_{k+2p^{m}}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle y_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for
i<D(p,m+1,k)
and a surjection for
i=D(p,m+1,k).

Proof.

Apply the argument of
Section

3.2
by replacing
x_{1}
and
y_{1}
with
x_{m}
and
y_{m}
, respectively.
∎

The case
m=1
in
Corollary

3.13
proves
Theorem

1.1
in the case that
M
is a surface and the homology is with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
coefficients. From
Corollary

3.13
,
we can also prove
Theorem

1.1
in the case that
M
is a surface and the homology is integral homology.

Proposition 3.14
.

Suppose that
M
is an open connected surface. The map

t_{\displaystyle x_{1}}\colon C_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})\to C_{i+1}(\text{Conf}_{k+2}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})

lifts to a map of singular chain complexes

t_{2}\colon C_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{Z})\to C_{i+1}(\text{Conf}_{k+2}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{Z})

and induces an isomorphism on integral homology for
i<D(3,1,k-1)+1=(2/3)k
and a surjection for
i=D(3,1,k-1)+1
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as the proof of
[
KM15
, Theorem 1.4]
. Suppose that
M
is orientable (if
M
is not orientable or odd dimensional, one modifies the argument by working with compactly supported cohomology with coefficients in the orientation systems of the configuration spaces instead—see
[
Seg79
, p. 71]
).
The class
x_{1}\in H_{1}(\text{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2});\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong H_{1}(\mathbb{R}P^{1};\mathbb{F}_{2})
comes from the class of a loop
\omega\in H_{1}(\mathbb{R}P^{1};\mathbb{Z})
in
\mathbb{R}P^{1}
. Therefore, we have a map

t_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}t_{\omega}\colon C_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{1}(M,(t_{1}));\mathbb{Z})\to C_{i+1}(\text{Cone}_{k+2}^{1}(M,(t_{1}));\mathbb{Z})

and we just need to show that this map induces an isomorphism on integral homology for
i<D(3,1,k-1)+1
and a surjection for
i=D(3,1,k-1)+1
.

It suffices to show that the homology
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}_{k+2}^{2}(M,(t_{1},t_{2}));\mathbb{Z})
vanishes for
i\leq D(3,1,k-1)+1
. We first show that it suffices to show that the homology
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}_{k+2}^{2}(M,(t_{1},t_{2}));\mathbb{F}_{p})
vanishes in the same range for all primes
p
by an argument in the proof of
[
GKR
, Theorem 18.2]
(see Reduction 2 and Reduction 3) which we now recall.

Let
\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2}
denote
\text{Cone}_{k+2}^{2}(M,(t_{1},t_{2}))
. By
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

3.8
, we can assume that
M
is the interior of a compact manifold with a finite handle decomposition with only one
0
-handle. Since
M
is the interior of a compact manifold with a finite handle decomposition, by
Lemma

3.10
,
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{Z})
is a finitely generated abelian group for all
i,k\geq 0
. Since
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})
is a quotient of
H_{i+1}(\text{Conf}_{k+2}(M);\mathbb{Z})
,
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})
is also a finitely generated abelian group.

By the universal coefficient theorem, we have the following short exact sequence:

0\to H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\to H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{p})\to\text{Tor}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{1}(H_{i}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{F}_{p})\to 0.

If
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})\neq 0
, then
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
is also non-zero for some prime
p
(here, we implicitly use that
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})
is a finitely generated abelian group). From the short exact sequence,
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
injects into
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{p})
, so
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{p})
must also be non-zero for some prime
p
. On the other hand, if
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{p})=0
for all primes
p
in a range then
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})
also vanishes in the same range (here, we again use that
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{Z})
is a finitely generated abelian group).

We now show that the homology
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{p})
vanishes for
i\leq D(3,1,k-1)+1
and for all primes
p
. We first deal with the case that
p
is an odd prime.
By
Proposition

3.6
, we have that
H_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{1}(M,(t_{1}));\mathbb{F}_{p})
and
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}_{k+2}^{1}(M,(t_{1}));\mathbb{F}_{p})
vanish for
i\leq D(p,1,k-1)
and
i\leq D(p,1,k+1)-1
, respectively. Since
D(3,1,k-1)\leq D(p,1,k-1)<D(p,1,k+1)-1
, the homology
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{p})
trivially vanishes for
i\leq D(3,1,k-1)+1
when
p
is an odd prime. When
p=2
, the homology
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{2})
vanishes for
i\leq D(2,2,k)
by
Corollary

3.13
. Since
D(3,1,k-1)+1\leq D(2,2,k)
, we have that the homology
H_{i+1}(\text{Cone}^{2}_{k+2};\mathbb{F}_{2})
vanishes for
i\leq D(3,1,k-1)+1
.
∎

3.3.
Secondary Homological Stability for Configuration Spaces of Higher Dimensional Manifolds

If
M
is an open connected manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
, Kupers–Miller showed that the relative homology
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M),\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{Z})
is a finite abelian
2
-group for
*\leq k
(see
Theorem

3.18
). Thus in this range, all secondary stability (and higher-order stability) results only concern
2
-torsion.
Consider the case
M
is
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The relative homology groups
H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
vanish only for
i\leq k/2
, so there might be non-trivial secondary homological stability for
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. To figure out whether there actually is secondary homological stability, we need the following description of
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
, which follows from combining
Theorem

2.6
,
Lemma

2.7
, and the fact that
\left[e,e\right]=0
when working with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
coefficients.

Theorem 3.15
.

Suppose that
n\geq 3
. Let
e\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
be the class of a point. Let
S
be the set of formal symbols constructed by iterated formal applications of the Dyer-Lashof operations
Q^{s}
and the top homology operation
\xi
to
e
(e.g.
Q^{2}Q^{1}e
is an element of
S
). We also allow zero applications of
Q^{s}
and
\xi
to
e
. Let
D\subset S
denote the subset of
S
containing elements of the form
e
and
Q^{2^{j}}Q^{2^{j-1}}\ldots Q^{2}Q^{1}e
for all non-negative
j
.
There is a subset
G_{n}\subset S
containing
D
such that
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
is isomorphic to the free graded commutative algebra on
G_{n}
.

We have the following analogue of
Proposition

2.11
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
e\in H_{0}(\text{Conf}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
be the class of a point, with
n>2
. Let
j
be a positive integer and let
A(j)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2^{j}-1
. Define a class
\omega_{j}\in H_{A(j)}(\text{Conf}_{A(j)+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
recursively by setting
\omega_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q^{1}e
and
\omega_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q^{A(j-1)+1}\omega_{j-1}
. Let
D(p,m,k)
denote the constant from
Proposition

2.11
.

Let
x
be a homology class in
H_{i+A(m-1)}(\text{Conf}_{k+A(m-1)+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
and suppose that
i\leq D(2,m,k)=A(m)k/(A(m)+1)
. Then
x
is in the ideal
(e,\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{m-1})
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
Proposition

2.11
. We say that a class
x\in H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
is in the
m-th unstable range
if
i\geq D(2,m,k)
. Clearly, for
j
in the range
0\leq j\leq m-1
, the classes
\omega_{j}
are not in the m-th unstable range. Let
R_{m}\subset H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
denote the complement of the ideal
(e,\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{m-1})
. It suffices to show that if
x
is in
R_{m}
, then
x
is in the m-th unstable range.

We need the following results on how homology operations affect the homological degree and number of particles of a class.

i)

For all
j\geq m
, the class
\omega_{j}
is in the m-th unstable range because
A(j)\geq A(m)
for all
j\geq m
.

ii)

For all
j
, the class
\xi\omega_{j}\in H_{2A(j)+n-1}(\text{Conf}_{2(A(j)+1)}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
is in the m-th unstable range. Since
A(j)=2^{j}-1
and
n-1
is at least
2
, we have that
2A(j)+n-1\geq 2(A(j)+1)
.

iii)

If
y\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
is in the m-th unstable range, then the classes
\xi y
and
Q^{s}y
, for all
s
in the range
a\leq s<a+n-1
, are also in the m-th unstable range. This is because
\xi y
and
Q^{s}y
are classes of degree
2a+n-1
and
a+s
, respectively, in
\text{Conf}_{2b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
2a+n-1,a+s\geq 2a\geq D(2,m,2b)
.

iv)

If
y\in H_{a}(\text{Conf}_{u}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
z\in H_{b}(\text{Conf}_{v}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
is in the m-th unstable range, then the class
yz
is also in the m-th unstable range. This is because if
y
and
z
are in the m-th unstable range, then
a\geq D(2,m,u)
and
b\geq D(2,m,v)
. Since
yz
is a class in
H_{a+b}(\text{Conf}_{u+v}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
, we have that
a+b\geq D(2,m,u+v)
.

v)

If
j
is in the range
1\leq j\leq m-1
and
s
is in the range
A(j)+1<s<A(j)+n-1
, then the class
Q^{s}\omega_{j}
is in the m-th unstable range. This is because
Q^{s}\omega_{j}
is a class in
H_{s+A(j)}(\text{Conf}_{2(A(j)+1)}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
s+A(j)
is at least
A(j+1)+1=2(A(j)+1)
in this range.

vi)

The classes
\xi e
and
Q^{s}e
for all
s
in the range
1<s<n-1
are in the m-th unstable range. This is because
\xi e
and
Q^{s}e
are classes in
H_{n-1}(\text{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
H_{s}(\text{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
respectively, and
n-1>s\geq 2
.

By
i)
, for all
j\geq m
the classes
\omega_{j}
are in the m-th unstable range so by
iii)
and
iv)
, applications of homology operations and products of these classes are also in the m-th unstable range as well. Therefore, the only classes that are possibly not in the m-th unstable range come from applications of homology operations to
e
or
\omega_{j}
for
j<m
. By
vi)
and
ii)
, the classes
\xi e
and
\xi\omega_{j}
are always in the m-th unstable range. Note that
Q^{0}e=e^{2}
,
Q^{1}e=\omega_{1}
, and
Q^{s}e
, for all
s
in the range
1<s<n-1
, is in the m-th unstable range by
vi)
(we ignore
Q^{s}e
for
s
negative because
Q^{s}e=0
in this range). Similarly,
Q^{s}\omega_{j}
is equal to
0
if
s<A(j)
and it is equal to
\omega_{j}^{2}
if
s=A(j)
. If
s=A(j)+1
, then
Q^{s}\omega_{j}
is equal to
\omega_{j+1}
, and for all
s
in the remaining range
A(j)+1<s\leq A(j)+n-1
, the class
Q^{s}\omega_{j}
is in the m-th unstable range by
v)
. Therefore, for
j\leq m-1
, the class
Q^{s}\omega_{j}
is in the m-th unstable range only when
s
is in the range
A(j)+1<s\leq A(j)+n-1
. Applications of homology operations and products of these
Q^{s}e
and
Q^{s}\omega_{j}
are also in the m-th unstable range by
iii)
and
iv)
. It follows that the classes that are not in the m-th unstable must be in the ideal
(e,\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{m-1})
.
∎

From
Proposition

3.16
, one can show:

Proposition 3.17
.

Suppose that
M
is an open connected manifold of dimension
n>2
.
Let
\omega_{j}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
denote the homology classes defined in
Proposition

3.16
. Let
D(p,m,k)
denote the constant from
Proposition

2.11
. Let
\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle\omega_{j}}))
denote the m-th iterated mapping cone associated to the collection
(t_{1},t_{\displaystyle\omega_{1}},\ldots,t_{\displaystyle\omega_{m-1}})
as defined in
Definition

2.10
.

(1)

The map

t_{1}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})
is an isomorphism for
i<k/2
and a surjection for
i=k/2
.

(2)

The map

t_{\displaystyle\omega_{m}}\colon H_{i}(\text{Cone}_{k}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle\omega_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H_{i+2^{m}-1}(\text{Cone}_{k+2^{m}}^{m}(M,(t_{\displaystyle\omega_{j}}));\mathbb{F}_{2})

is an isomorphism for
i<D(2,m+1,k)
and a surjection for
i=D(2,m+1,k).

Proof.

This immediately follows by applying the arguments of
Section

3.2
and the range is obtained from
Proposition

3.16
just like in
Corollary

2.12
.
∎

The case
m=1
in
Proposition

3.17
proves
Theorem

1.1
in the case that the dimension of
M
is greater than
2
and the homology is with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
coefficients. From
Proposition

3.17
,
we can also prove
Theorem

1.1
in the case that the dimension of
M
is greater than
2
and the homology is integral homology. We need the following result on homological stability of
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)
when the dimension of
M
is greater than
2
.

Theorem 3.18

(
[
Seg79
, Proposition A.1]
,
[
KM15
, Theorem 1.4]
)
.

Let
M
be an open connected manifold of dimension
n>2
and let
R
be a ring such that
2
is a unit.
The stabilization map

t_{1}\colon H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M);R)\to H_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);R)

is an isomorphism for
i<f(R,k)
and a surjection for
i=f(R,k)
,
where

f(R,k)=\begin{cases}k&\text{if }2\text{ is a unit in }R\\
k/2&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proposition 3.19
.

Suppose that
M
is an open connected manifold of dimension
n>2
. The map

t_{\displaystyle\omega_{1}}\colon C_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})\to C_{i+1}(\text{Conf}_{k+2}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{F}_{2})

lifts to a map of singular chain complexes

t_{\displaystyle\omega_{1}}\colon C_{i}(\text{Conf}_{k}(M),\text{Conf}_{k-1}(M);\mathbb{Z})\to C_{i+1}(\text{Conf}_{k+2}(M),\text{Conf}_{k+1}(M);\mathbb{Z})

and induces an isomorphism on integral homology for
i<D(2,2,k)
and a surjection for
i=D(2,2,k)
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as the proof of
Proposition

3.14
. The only difference is that we have to apply
Proposition

3.17
and the range
f(R,k)
from
Theorem

3.18
when proving that the
t_{\displaystyle\omega_{1}}
induces an isomorphism on relative integral homology groups in a range.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

The classes
\omega_{j}\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
for
j>1
do not lift to classes in
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
. To see this, consider the mod-
2
Bockstein spectral sequence on
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
. If
\omega_{j}
lifted to a class in
H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
, then it would survive to the
E^{\infty}
-page of this spectral sequence. The
d^{1}
-differential of this spectral sequence is the Bockstein homomorphism
\beta\colon H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})\to H_{*-1}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})
coming from the short exact sequence
0\to\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/4\to\mathbb{Z}/2\to 0
. We now show that
\beta(\omega_{j})
is non-zero for
j>1
, and so
\omega_{j}
does survive to the
E^{\infty}
-page.

Recall that
\beta
is dual to the Steenrod square operation
\text{Sq}^{1}
. Therefore, by the Nishida relation for Dyer-Lashof operations (see Property 6 in
[
CLM76
, 214]
), for any
s>0
, we have that
\beta Q^{s}=(s-1)Q^{s-1}
. It follows that for
j>1
,

\beta(\omega_{j})=\beta(Q^{2^{j-1}}\omega_{j-1})=Q^{2^{j-1}-1}\omega_{j-1}.

Since
\omega_{j-1}
is a class of degree
2^{j-1}-1
, by Property 2 in
[
CLM76
, 213]
),
Q^{2^{j-1}-1}\omega_{j-1}=\omega_{j-1}^{2}
, which is non-zero for
j>1
.

We now prove
Theorem

1.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

Theorem

1.1
immediately follows from
Corollary

3.13
,
Proposition

3.14
,
Proposition

3.17
, and
Proposition

3.19
in the case
m=1
.
∎

4.
Models for the Configuration Spaces of a Manifold and for the Configuration Spaces of a Disk

In this section, we give various models of configuration spaces.
In
Section

4.1
, we begin by introducing a topological monoid and a topological module over it
and we use these spaces to produce a model of
\text{Conf}(M)
. Then in
Section

4.2
, we work with
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
, now viewed as a module over
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty))
.
Roughly speaking, we use free modules over
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty))
to construct a model for
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
. This model for
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
has the additional structure of being a module over
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty))
and is different from our model for
\text{Conf}(M)
.
Ultimately, the stabilization map we construct on
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)
comes from a stabilization map on
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)
and we will define a stabilization map on the latter by using this other model for
\text{Conf}(D^{n})
.

4.1.
Models for the Configuration Spaces of a Manifold

In this subsection, we describe two related models of configuration spaces for two different contexts.
The first model associates to a space
N
a topological monoid
\text{MConf}(N)
whose underlying space is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(N\times(0,\infty))
. The second model associates to a manifold
M_{1}
with boundary and an ordered
m
-tuple
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
of disjoint subspaces contained in
\partial M_{1}
, a space
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{\sqcup_{j}N_{j}}(M_{1})
whose underlying space is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(M_{1})
and which is a module over
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
.
We also construct a semi-simplicial space

\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})

which is
built from the spaces
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
,
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{\sqcup_{j}N_{j}}(M_{1})
, and
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{\sqcup_{j}N_{j}}(M_{2})
.
Its geometric realization is a space which is weakly equivalent to
\text{Conf}(M_{1}\cup_{\sqcup_{j}N_{j}}M_{2})
and we will use this semi-simplicial space to define a stabilization map in
Section

5.3
.

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose that
N
is a space and that
a\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
\text{sh}_{a}\colon\text{Conf}(N\times(-\infty,\infty))\to\text{Conf}(N\times(-\infty,\infty))
be the “shift by
a
” map

\displaystyle\text{sh}_{a}\colon\text{Conf}(N\times(-\infty,\infty))

\displaystyle\to\text{Conf}({N}\times(-\infty,\infty))

\displaystyle\{(q_{1},u_{1}),\ldots,(q_{k},u_{k})\}

\displaystyle\mapsto\{(q_{1},a+u_{1}),\ldots,(q_{k},a+u_{k})\}.

Definition 4.2
.

Suppose that
N
is a space and
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\text{sc}_{a}\colon\text{Conf}(N\times(-\infty,\infty))\to\text{Conf}(N\times(-\infty,\infty))
be
the “scale by
a
” map

\displaystyle\text{sc}_{a}\colon\text{Conf}(N\times(-\infty,\infty))

\displaystyle\to\text{Conf}({N}\times(-\infty,\infty))

\displaystyle\{(q_{1},u_{1}),\ldots,(q_{k},u_{k})\}

\displaystyle\mapsto\{(q_{1},a\cdot u_{1}),\ldots,(q_{k},a\cdot u_{k})\}.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
N
be a space. Define the
strict monoid replacement

\text{MConf}(N)
for the configuration space
\text{Conf}(N\times(0,\infty))
to be the subspace of
\text{Conf}(N\times(0,\infty))\times\left[0,\infty\right)
consisting of pairs
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
with
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Conf}(N\times(0,\lambda))\subset\text{Conf}(N\times\mathbb{R})
for
\lambda>0
and
(\{\emptyset\},0)
for
\lambda=0
.

Addition of elements in
\text{MConf}(N)
is given by

\displaystyle-\cdot-\colon\text{MConf}(N)\times\text{MConf}(N)

\displaystyle\to\text{MConf}(N)

\displaystyle\big((\mathcal{C}_{1},\lambda_{1}),(\mathcal{C}_{2},\lambda_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathcal{C}_{1}\cup\text{sh}_{\lambda_{1}}(\mathcal{C}_{2}),\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}).

The space
\text{MConf}(N)
is a topological associative monoid with unit
(\{\emptyset\},0)
. Given an
m
-tuple of spaces
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
, we use the notation
(\vec{\mathcal{C}},\vec{\lambda})
to denote an element of
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
, with

\vec{\mathcal{C}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\vec{\mathcal{C}}(1),\ldots,\vec{\mathcal{C}}(m))\in\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{Conf}(N_{j}\times(0,\infty))\text{ and }\vec{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\vec{\lambda}(1),\ldots,\vec{\lambda}(m))\in\left[0,\infty\right)^{m}

satisfying the condition that
(\vec{\mathcal{C}}(j),\vec{\lambda}(j))
is an element of
\text{MConf}(N_{j})
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
.

Remark 4.4
.

The motivation behind
Definition

4.3
is a construction, due to Moore, which turns the loop space
\Omega N
of a space
N
into a strict monoid instead of a monoid up to homotopy (see
[
CM95
, p. 529–530]

for the definition of the Moore loop space).
See
[
Hat
, p. 9]
for applications of the strict monoid replacement
\text{MConf}(N)
to the Barrat–Priddy–Quillen theorem.

Definition 4.5
.

Suppose that
M
is a manifold with boundary and that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
is an
m
-tuple of disjoint subspaces
N_{i}\subset\partial M
. Let
N
denote
\bigsqcup\limits_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}
.
Define the
strict module replacement

\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)
over the strict monoid replacement
\prod\limits_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
for the configuration space
\text{Conf}(M)
to be the subspace of

\text{Conf}\Big(M\cup_{N}\big({N}\times\left[0,\infty\right)\big)\Big)\times\left[0,\infty\right)^{m}
consisting of elements

(\mathcal{C},\vec{\lambda})
, with
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Conf}\Big(M\cup_{N}\big({N}\times\left[0,\infty\right)\big)\Big)
and
\vec{\lambda}=(\vec{\lambda}(1),\ldots,\vec{\lambda}(m))\in[0,\infty)^{m}
satisfying the condition that

\mathcal{C}\in\text{Conf}\Big(M\cup_{N}\big(\bigsqcup\limits_{j=1,\ldots,m,\text{ with }\vec{\lambda}(j)\neq 0}^{m}N_{j}\times[0,\vec{\lambda}(j))\big)\setminus(\bigcup\limits_{k=1\ldots,m,\text{ with }\vec{\lambda}(k)=0}^{m}N_{k})\Big).

If
m=1
,
we denote an element of
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{N}(M)
by
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
.

Let

\iota_{j}\colon N_{j}\times(0,\infty)\hookrightarrow N_{j}\times(0,\infty)\subset M\cup_{N}({N}\times\left[0,\infty\right))

be the inclusion under the identity map and let

\text{Conf}(\iota_{j})\colon\text{Conf}(N_{j}\times(0,\infty))\to\text{Conf}\big(M\cup_{N}({N}\times\left[0,\infty\right))\big)

denote the induced map of configuration spaces.
The map

\displaystyle m_{r}\colon\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)\times\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})

\displaystyle\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)

\displaystyle\big((\mathcal{C}_{1},\vec{\lambda}_{1}),(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{2},\vec{\lambda}_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}\text{Conf}(\iota_{j})\big(\text{sh}_{\displaystyle\vec{\lambda}_{1}(j)}(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(j))\big)\cup\mathcal{C}_{1},\vec{\lambda}_{1}+\vec{\lambda}_{2}\big)

gives the space
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)
the structure of a right module over the monoid
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
(see
Figure

6
).

Figure 6.
The right
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
-module structure of
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{\sqcup_{j}N_{j}}(M)

Similarly, the map

\displaystyle m_{l}\colon\big(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})\big)\times\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)

\displaystyle\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)

\big((\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\vec{\lambda}_{1}),(\mathcal{C}_{2},\vec{\lambda}_{2})\big)\mapsto\big(\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}\text{Conf}(\iota_{j})\big(\text{sh}_{\displaystyle\vec{\lambda}_{1}(j)+\vec{\lambda}_{2}(j)}\text{sc}_{-1}(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{1}(j))\big)\cup\mathcal{C}_{2},\vec{\lambda}_{1}+\vec{\lambda}_{2}\big)

gives the space
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)
the structure of a left module over the monoid
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
. When
m=1
,
we will write
\iota
instead of
\iota_{1}
.

Definition 4.6
.

Given an element
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
of
\text{MConf}(N)
or an element
(\mathcal{C},\vec{\lambda})
of
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)
, we define a
point
of
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
or
(\mathcal{C},\vec{\lambda})
to be any point of the configuration
\mathcal{C}
.
We will denote a point of
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
or
(\mathcal{C},\vec{\lambda})
by
(q,u)
, with
u\in\left[0,\lambda\right)
and
q\in N
for
u>0
and
q\in M
for
u=0
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that
M
is a manifold with boundary and that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
is an
m
-tuple of disjoint subspaces
N_{i}\subset\partial M
. Let
N
denote
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}
.
The spaces
\text{MConf}(N)
and
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)
are homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(N\times(0,1))
and
\text{Conf}\big(M\cup_{N}({N}\times\left[0,1\right))\big)
, respectively.

Proof.

There is a deformation retract of the Moore loop space to the loop space (see, for example, the proof of
[
CM95
, Proposition 5.1.1]
) and a similar deformation retract of
\text{MConf}(N)
onto
\text{Conf}(N\times(0,1))\times\{1\}
works in our setting.
The argument for
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M)
is similar so we omit it.

Let
\text{MConf}^{\geq 1}(N)\subset\text{MConf}(N)
denote the subspace of pairs
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)\in\text{MConf}(N)
with
\lambda\geq 1
. A deformation retract
H\colon\left[0,1\right]\times\text{MConf}(N)\to\text{MConf}(N)
to
\text{MConf}^{\geq 1}(N)
is given by the following formula:

H\big(s,(\mathcal{C},\lambda)\big)=\begin{cases}(\mathcal{C},s+\lambda)&s+\lambda\leq 1\\
(\mathcal{C},1)&\lambda\leq 1\text{ and }s+\lambda\geq 1\\
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)&\lambda\geq 1.\end{cases}

Let
f\colon\left[0,1\right]\times\left[1,\infty\right)\to\left[0,\infty\right)
be the function sending
(s,\lambda)
to
\displaystyle\frac{(1-s)\lambda+s}{\lambda}
. The map

\displaystyle G\colon\left[0,1\right]\times\text{MConf}^{\geq 1}(N)

\displaystyle\to\text{MConf}^{\geq 1}(N)

\displaystyle(s,(\mathcal{C},\lambda))

\displaystyle\mapsto(\text{sc}_{f(s,\lambda)}(\mathcal{C}),(1-s)\lambda+s)

is a deformation retract of
\text{MConf}^{\geq 1}(N)
to
\text{Conf}(N\times(0,1))\times\{1\}
.
∎

Definition 4.8

(Bar Construction)
.

Suppose that
V
is a monoid,
U
is a right
V
-module, and
W
is a left
V
-module. The
bar construction

B_{\bullet}(U,V,W)
is the semi-simplicial space whose
p
-simplices
B_{p}(U,V,W)
are
U\times V^{p}\times W
. Given
u\in U
,
v_{1},\ldots,v_{p}\in V
, and
w\in W
, the face maps
d_{i}\colon B_{p}(U,V,W)\to B_{p-1}(U,V,W)
for
0\leq i\leq p
send
(u,v_{1},\ldots,v_{p},w)
to

\displaystyle d_{0}(u,v_{1},\ldots,v_{p},w)

\displaystyle=(m_{r}(u,v_{1}),v_{2},\ldots,v_{p},w)

\displaystyle d_{i}(u,v_{1},\ldots,v_{p},w)

\displaystyle=(u,v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1},v_{i}\cdot v_{i+1},v_{i+2},\ldots,v_{p},w)\text{ for }0<i<p

\displaystyle d_{p}(u,v_{1},\ldots,v_{p},w)

\displaystyle=(u,v_{1},\ldots,v_{p-1},m_{l}(v_{p},w)),

where
m_{r}\colon U\times V\to U
is the right module map of
U
and
m_{l}\colon V\times W\to W
is the left module map of
W
.

Suppose that
M_{1}
and
M_{1}
are manifolds with boundary and that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
is an
m
-tuple of disjoint subspaces
N_{i}
contained in
\partial M_{1}
and
\partial M_{2}
. Let
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}
.
Since
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M_{1})
is a left module over the monoid
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j})
, and
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M_{2})
) is a right module over it, we can take the bar construction

B_{\bullet}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M_{1}),\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j}),\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M_{2})).

Definition 4.9
.

Suppose that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are two manifolds with boundary and that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
is an
m
-tuple of disjoint subspaces contained in
\partial M_{1}
and
\partial M_{2}
. Let
N
denote
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}
. Define
\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
to be the semi-simplicial space

\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}B_{\bullet}\big(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M_{1}),\prod_{j=1}^{m}\text{MConf}(N_{j}),\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,m}_{N}(M_{2})\big).

Let
\mathcal{X}_{k,\bullet}^{m}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\subset\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
(respectively,
\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\subset\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
) denote the semi-simplicial space whose
p
-simplices consist of elements

\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\vec{\lambda}_{0}),(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\vec{\lambda}_{1}),\ldots,(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{p},\vec{\lambda}_{p}),(\mathcal{C}_{p+1},\vec{\lambda}_{p+1})\big)\in\mathcal{X}^{m}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
such that

|\mathcal{C}_{0}|+|\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{1}|+\ldots+|\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{p}|+|\mathcal{C}_{p+1}|=k

(respectively,
\sum_{j=0}^{p+1}\vec{\lambda}_{j}(i)=1
for all
i=1,\ldots,m
).

We want to compare the geometric realizations
\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\|
and
\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|
. We will need the following well-known fact concerning the geometric realization of a semi-simplicial space:

Theorem 4.10

(e.g.
[
ER19
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Let
g_{\bullet}\colon Y_{\bullet}\to Z_{\bullet}
be a map of semi-simplicial spaces which is a levelwise weak homotopy equivalence, i.e.
g_{p}\colon Y_{p}\to Z_{p}
is a weak homotopy equivalence for all
p
. Then
\|g_{\bullet}\|\colon\|Y_{\bullet}\|\to\|Z_{\bullet}\|
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
g_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\to\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
be the map of semi-simplicial spaces coming from the natural inclusion
\mathcal{X}^{m}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{m}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
. The map on geometric realizations

\|g_{\bullet}\|\colon\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\|\to\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

For simplicity, we prove this result only for the case
m=1
. Given
\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0}),\ldots,(\mathcal{C}_{p+1},\lambda_{p+1})\big)\in\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
, let
\lambda\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{j=0}^{p+1}\lambda_{j}
.
Let
f_{p}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\to\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}
denote the map

\displaystyle f_{p}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}

\displaystyle\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0}),\ldots,(\mathcal{C}_{p+1},\lambda_{p+1})\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\big(\text{sc}_{1/(1+\lambda)}(\mathcal{C}_{0}),\frac{1+\lambda_{0}}{1+\lambda}\big),\big(\text{sc}_{1/(1+\lambda)}(\mathcal{C}_{1}),\frac{\lambda_{1}}{1+\lambda}\big),\ldots,\big(\text{sc}_{1/(1+\lambda)}(\mathcal{C}_{p+1}),\frac{\lambda_{p+1}}{1+\lambda}\big)\big).

and let
g_{p}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
be the inclusion map. The maps
f_{p}
and
g_{p}
assemble to define maps of semi-simplicial spaces
f_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\to\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}
and
g_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\to\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
.

We now check that the map
f_{p}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\to\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}
is a homotopy equivalence. Let
r\colon\left[0,1\right]\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be the function sending
(t,a)
to
1/(1+at)
. The map

\displaystyle H\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\times\left[0,1\right]

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})

\displaystyle\big(((\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0}),\ldots,(\mathcal{C}_{p+1},\lambda_{p+1})),t\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\big(\text{sc}_{r(t,\lambda)}(\mathcal{C}_{0}),\frac{t+\lambda_{0}}{1+t\lambda}\big),\big(\text{sc}_{r(t,\lambda)}(\mathcal{C}_{1}),\frac{\lambda_{1}}{1+t\lambda}\big),\ldots,\big(\text{sc}_{r(t,\lambda)}(\mathcal{C}_{p+1}),\frac{\lambda_{p+1}}{1+t\lambda}\big)\big)

defines a homotopy equivalence between
\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
and
\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}
. Since
f_{p}\colon\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\to\mathcal{X}^{1}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}
is a homotopy equivalence for all
p
, by
Theorem

4.10
, the map

\|f_{\bullet}\|\colon\|\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|\to\|\mathcal{X}^{1}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\|

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

The proof for
m>1
is similar to the case
m=1
–one just needs to rescale additional terms.
∎

Suppose that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are manifolds with boundary and that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
is an
m
-tuple of disjoint subspaces contained in
\partial M_{1}
and
\partial M_{2}
. Let
N\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}
.
Let
M
be the space

M_{1}\cup_{N}({N}\times[0,1]^{m})\cup_{N}M_{2}
, where
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
attached along
{N}\times\{(0,0,\ldots,0)\}
and
{N}\times\{(1,1,\ldots,1)\}
, respectively.
We now introduce a semi-simplicial model for
\text{Conf}(M)
.

Definition 4.12
.

Suppose that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are two manifolds with boundary and that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{m})
is an
m
-tuple of disjoint subspaces contained in
\partial M_{1}
and
\partial M_{2}
. Let
N
denote
\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}
.
Let
M
be the space
M_{1}\cup_{N}({N\times\{0\}}\times[0,1])\cup_{N\times\{1\}}M_{2}
, where
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
attached along
{N}\times\{0\}
and
{N}\times\{1\}
, respectively.
Let
B_{\bullet}(M,N)
denote the semi-simplicial space with

B_{p}(M,N)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left\{(\mathcal{C};\vec{\lambda}_{0},\ldots,\vec{\lambda}_{p})\in\text{Conf}(M)\times(0,1)^{m(p+1)}:\begin{aligned} &\vec{\lambda}_{0}(j)<\cdots<\vec{\lambda}_{p+1}(j)\text{ and }\mathcal{C}\cap N_{j}\times\{\vec{\lambda}_{i}(j)\}=\emptyset\\
&\text{ for all }i=1,\ldots,p,\text{ and }j=1,\ldots,m\end{aligned}\right\}

and with
d_{i}\colon B_{p}(M,N)\to B_{p-1}(M,N)
the map that forgets
\vec{\lambda_{i}}
.

Definition 4.13
.

An
augmented semi-simplicial space
is a triple
(Z_{\bullet},Z_{-1},\epsilon)
consisting of a semi-simplicial space
Z_{\bullet}
, a space
Z_{-1}
, and a map
\epsilon\colon Z_{0}\to Z_{-1}
such that
\epsilon d_{0}=\epsilon d_{1}
. The map
\epsilon\colon Z_{0}\to Z_{-1}
is called the
augmentation map
.

An augmented semi-simplicial space
(Z_{\bullet},Z_{-1},\epsilon)
induces a map

\|\epsilon\|\colon\|Z_{\bullet}\|\to Z_{-1}
.

Definition 4.14
.

A
semi-simplicial resolution
of a space
Z_{-1}
is an augmented semi-simplicial space
(Z_{\bullet},Z_{-1},\epsilon)
such that the induced map
\|\epsilon\|\colon\|Z_{\bullet}\|\to Z_{-1}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Let
\epsilon\colon B_{0}(M,N)\to\text{Conf}(M)
be the map which forgets
\vec{\lambda}_{0}
. The triple
(B_{\bullet}(M,N),\text{Conf}(M),\epsilon)
is an augmented semi-simplicial space. To show that
(B_{\bullet}(M,N),\text{Conf}(M),\epsilon)
is a semi-simplicial resolution of
\text{Conf}(M)
, we now introduce a technique of
[
GR14
, Section 6.2]
.

Definition 4.15
.

An
augmented topological flag complex
is an augmented semi-simplicial space
(Z_{\bullet},Z_{-1},\epsilon)
such that

(1)

The map
Z_{p}\to Z_{0}\times_{Z_{-1}}Z_{0}\times_{Z_{-1}}\cdots\times_{Z_{-1}}Z_{0}
to the
(p+1)
-fold product–which takes a
p
-simplex to its
(p+1)
vertices–is a homeomorphism onto its image, which is an open subset, and

(2)

A tuple
(v_{0},\ldots,v_{p+1})\in Z_{0}\times_{Z_{-1}}Z_{0}\times_{Z_{-1}}\cdots\times_{Z_{-1}}Z_{0}
lies in
Z_{p+1}
if and only if
(z_{i},z_{j})\in Z_{1}
for all
i<j
.

Theorem 4.16

(
[
GR14
, Theorem 6.2]
)
.

Let
(Z_{\bullet},Z_{-1},\epsilon)
be an augmented topological flag complex. Suppose that

(1)

The map
\epsilon\colon Z_{0}\to Z_{-1}
has local sections (in the sense that given any
z\in Z_{0}
, there is a neighborhood
U\subset Z_{-1}
of
\epsilon(z)
and a section
s\colon U\to Z_{0}
with
s(\epsilon(z))=z
),

(2)

The map
\epsilon\colon Z_{0}\to Z_{-1}
is surjective, and

(3)

For any
x\in Z_{-1}
and any (non-empty) finite set
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\}\subset\epsilon^{-1}(x)
, there exists a
z\in\epsilon^{-1}(x)
with
(z_{i},z)\in Z_{1}
for all
i
.

Then
\|\epsilon\|\colon\|Z_{\bullet}\|\to Z_{-1}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proposition 4.17
.

The map
\|\epsilon\|\colon\|B_{\bullet}(M,N)\|\to\text{Conf}(M)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Our argument is the same as the proof of
[
Ran13
, Proposition 2.1]
. We want to apply
Theorem

4.16
. The augmented semi-simplicial space
(B_{\bullet}(M,N),\text{Conf}(M),\epsilon)
is an augmented topological flag complex. Conditions
1
and
2
are clear. For Condition
3
, given a configuration
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Conf}(M)
, the set

\epsilon^{-1}(\mathcal{C})\cong\{\vec{\lambda}\in(0,1)^{m}:\mathcal{C}\cap N_{j}\times\{\vec{\lambda}(j)\}=\emptyset\text{ for all }j=1,\ldots,m\}

is infinite since
\mathcal{C}
has finitely many points. Therefore, for any finite subset
\{\vec{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\vec{\lambda}_{n}\}\subset\{\vec{\lambda}\in(0,1)^{m}:\mathcal{C}\cap N_{j}\times\{\vec{\lambda}(j)\}=\emptyset\text{ for all }j=1,\ldots,m\}
we can pick an element
\vec{\lambda}\in\epsilon^{-1}(\mathcal{C})
such that
\vec{\lambda}_{i}(j)<\vec{\lambda}(j)
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
and
j=1,\ldots,m
, so Condition
3
holds. By
Theorem

4.16
, the map
\|\epsilon\|\colon\|B_{\bullet}(M,N)\|\to\text{Conf}(M)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

The space
\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|
is weakly homotopy equivalent to
\|B_{\bullet}(M,N)\|
.

Proof.

Let
W_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\subset\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}
denote the semi-simplicial space whose
p
-simplices consist of elements
\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\vec{\lambda}_{0}),(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\vec{\lambda}_{1}),\ldots,(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{p},\vec{\lambda}_{p}),(\mathcal{C}_{p+1},\vec{\lambda}_{p+1})\big)\in\mathcal{X}^{m}_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}
such that
\vec{\lambda}_{i}(j)>0
for all
i=1,\ldots,p
and all
j
. We have a weak homotopy equivalence

\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\|\to\|W_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|.

Given
\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\vec{\lambda}_{0}),(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\vec{\lambda}_{1}),\ldots,(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{p},\vec{\lambda}_{p}),(\mathcal{C}_{p+1},\vec{\lambda}_{p+1})\big)\in W_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})
, let
\vec{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{k=0}^{i}\vec{\lambda}_{k}
. We can view
\bigcup_{i=0}^{p+1}\mathcal{C}_{i}
as an element of
\text{Conf}(M)
under the identifications of

(1)

\sqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}\times[0,\vec{\lambda}_{0}(j)]
with
\sqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}\times[0,\vec{\lambda}_{0}(j)]\subset M
via the identity map,

(2)

\sqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}\times[0,\vec{\lambda}_{i}(j)]
with
\sqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}\times[\vec{\lambda}_{i-1}^{\prime}(j),\vec{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}(j)]\subset M
,
for
i=1,\ldots,p
, via the map
(n,t)\mapsto(n,\vec{\lambda}_{i-1}^{\prime}(j)+t)
, and

(3)

\sqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}\times[0,\vec{\lambda}_{p+1}(j)]
with
\sqcup_{j=1}^{m}N_{j}\times[\vec{\lambda}_{p+1}^{\prime}(j),1]\subset M
via the map
(n,t)\mapsto(n,1-t)
(see
Figure

7
).

Figure 7.
The map
f_{\bullet}\colon W_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\to B_{\bullet}(M,N)

There are maps

\displaystyle f_{p}\colon W_{p}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})

\displaystyle\to B_{p}(M,N)

\displaystyle\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\vec{\lambda}_{0}),(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\vec{\lambda}_{1}),\ldots,(\vec{\mathcal{C}}_{p},\vec{\lambda}_{p}),(\mathcal{C}_{p+1},\vec{\lambda}_{p+1})\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\bigcup_{i=0}^{p+1}\mathcal{C}_{i};\vec{\lambda}^{\prime}_{0},\vec{\lambda}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\vec{\lambda}^{\prime}_{p})

that assemble to define a map of semi-simplicial spaces
f_{\bullet}\colon W_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\to B_{\bullet}(M,N)
. Since the map
f_{p}
is a homeomorphism for all
p
, the induced map
\|f_{\bullet}\|\colon\|W_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|\to\|B_{\bullet}(M,N)\|
is a weak homotopy equivalence by
Theorem

4.10
. Since the composition of weak homotopy equivalences is a weak homotopy equivalence, the map
\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\|\to\|B_{\bullet}(M,N)\|
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
∎

Corollary 4.19
.

There is a weak homotopy equivalence
\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|\to\text{Conf}(M)
.

Proof.

We have the following composition

\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|\to\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})^{+}\|\to\|B_{\bullet}(M,N)\|\to\text{Conf}(M)

of weak homotopy equivalences by
Lemma

4.11
,
Lemma

4.18
, and
Proposition

4.17
. Since the composition of weak homotopy equivalences is a weak homotopy equivalence, the map
\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|\to\text{Conf}(M)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
∎

Remark 4.20
.

The weak homotopy equivalence

\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|\to\text{Conf}(M)

also follows from the theory of factorization homology. Factorization homology, also known as topological chiral homology or manifoldic homology, is a gadget which takes as inputs an
n
-manifold
M
and an algebra
A
over the framed little
n
-disk operad
fE_{n}
and outputs an object
\int_{M}A
in a category (see
[
AF15
]
or
[
Mil15
, Section 2]
for more information about factorization homology). When the
fE_{n}
-algebra is
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the factorization homology
\int_{M}A
is
\text{Conf}(M)
. The weak homotopy equivalence

\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({M_{1}},{N},{M_{2}})\|\to\text{Conf}(M)

is a special case of excision for factorization homology due to Ayala–Francis (see
[
AF15
, Lemma 3.18]
). One could prove this weak homotopy equivalence by quoting their result, but it is easier to prove this weak homotopy equivalence directly than to compare our framework with theirs.
Recent work of An–Drummond-Cole–Knudsen has also obtained a similar excision result for configuration spaces in finite CW complexes (see
[
ADK19
, Theorem 3.20]
).

4.2.
A Model of the Configuration Space of the Disk

By
Corollary

4.19
, there is a weak homotopy equivalence

\|\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}\bar{D}^{n}},{\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}S^{n-1}},{M\setminus\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}D^{n}})\|\to\text{Conf}(M).

We will use
\mathcal{X}^{m}_{\bullet}({\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}\bar{D}^{n}},{\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}S^{n-1}},{M\setminus\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{m}D^{n}})
to construct a semi-simplicial model for
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)
in
Section

5.3
. As a consequence of this model, to define a stabilization map on
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)
, it will suffice to construct a stabilization map on
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D});R)
that is a map of
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
-modules. Our first step in constructing such a stabilization map on
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D});R)
is to build a model for
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D})
using free
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-modules and we will do that in this subsection.

Definition 4.21
.

Define
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}\subset\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
to be the submodule consisting of elements
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)\in\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
such that at most one point of
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is in
\bar{D}^{n}
.

Lemma 4.22
.

The inclusion
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}\hookrightarrow\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is a map of
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-modules and a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

It is clear that
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}\hookrightarrow\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is a map of
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-modules. Let
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}\subset\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
denote the subspace of configurations

\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(q_{1},u_{1}),\ldots,(q_{k},u_{k})\}\in\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that at most one point of
\mathcal{C}
is in the closed disk
\bar{B}(0,1)
of radius
1
centered at the origin.
We think of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
\bar{B}(0,1)\cup_{S^{n-1}}S^{n-1}\times\left[0,\infty\right)
. From the inclusion

\bar{B}(0,1)\cup_{S^{n-1}}S^{n-1}\times\left[0,\lambda\right)\to\bar{B}(0,1)\cup_{S^{n-1}}S^{n-1}\times\left[0,\infty\right),

we have a map

\displaystyle\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{B}(0,1))

\displaystyle\to\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C},\lambda)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{C}

which restricts to a map
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{B}(0,1))^{\leq 1}\to\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}
. We also have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\centering\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{B}(0,1))^{\leq 1}\@add@centering}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{B}(0,1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).}

By
Lemma

4.7
,
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
By the argument in the proof of
Lemma

4.7
,
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
. Therefore,
to show that
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{B}(0,1))^{\leq 1}
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{B}(0,1))

it suffices to show that the right vertical map
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}\hookrightarrow\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a homotopy equivalence.

We prove that
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by pushing points in a configuration
\mathcal{C}\in\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
off the ball
B(0,1)
. We can do this by scaling a configuration
\mathcal{C}
by a function
f
depending on the configuration’s second closest point to the origin, which we now define.
Given
\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\}\in\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, reorder
q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}
so that
\lvert q_{1}\rvert\leq\lvert q_{2}\rvert\ldots\leq\lvert q_{k}\rvert
and let
f\colon\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to(0,\infty)
be
the “second closest point” function

f(\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }k=0,1,\\
2/\lvert q_{2}\rvert&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Define
F\colon\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}
to be
the scaling function

\displaystyle F\colon\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle\to\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}\}

\displaystyle\mapsto\text{sc}_{f(\mathcal{C})}(\mathcal{C})=\{f(\mathcal{C})\cdot q_{1},\ldots,f(\mathcal{C})\cdot q_{k}\}

Let
G\colon\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}\to\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the inclusion map. There is a homotopy

\displaystyle H\colon\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\left[0,1\right]

\displaystyle\to\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C},t)

\displaystyle\mapsto\text{sc}_{f(\mathcal{C})(1-t)+t}(\mathcal{C})

from
G\circ F
to
id
.
By restriction of the map
H
to
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}\times\left[0,1\right]
, the map
H
also gives a homotopy from
F\circ G
to
id
. Therefore,
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

We now introduce a semi-simplicial model for
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
.

Definition 4.23
.

Suppose that
N
is a space. Let
\text{MConf}(N)_{k}\subset\text{MConf}(N)
denote the subspace of elements
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)\in\text{MConf}(N)
such that the cardinality of the configuration
\mathcal{C}
is equal to
k
.
If
M
is a manifold with boundary and
N
is contained in
\partial M
, define
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{N}(M)_{k}
similarly and let
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{N}(M)_{\leq k}
denote the subspace of elements
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)\in\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{N}(M)
such that the cardinality of the configuration
\mathcal{C}
is at most
k
.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
Y_{\bullet}
denote the semi-simplicial space whose
p
-simplices are

Y_{p}=\begin{cases}\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})_{\leq 1}\times\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})&p=0\\
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})_{1}\times\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})&p=1\\
\emptyset&\text{for }p>1.\end{cases}

The face maps
d_{0},d_{1}\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{0}
are defined as follows (see
Figure

8
): given
\xi=\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0}),(\mathcal{C}_{1},\lambda_{1})\big)\in\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})_{1}\times\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
, let

\displaystyle d_{0}(\xi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\big((\emptyset,0),(\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0})\cdot(\mathcal{C}_{1},\lambda_{1})\big)=\big((\emptyset,0),(\text{sh}_{\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{C}_{1})\cup\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1})\big),\text{ and}

\displaystyle d_{1}(\xi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle\big((\text{Conf}(\iota)(\mathcal{C}_{0}),\lambda_{0}),(\mathcal{C}_{1},\lambda_{1})\big),

where
\text{sh}_{\lambda_{0}}
is “the shift by
\lambda_{0}
” map from
Definition

4.1
, and
\iota\colon S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty)\hookrightarrow\bar{D}^{n}\cup_{S^{n-1}}(S^{n-1}\times\left[0,\infty\right))
is the inclusion under the identity map from
Definition

4.5
.
Let
Y
denote the geometric realization
\|Y_{\bullet}\|
.

Figure 8.
The face maps
d_{0},d_{1}\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{0}

The space
Y
can be described as a homotopy coequalizer
\text{hocoeq}(d_{0},d_{1}\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{0})
. Note that up to homotopy,
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
can be viewed as free right
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-modules over
S^{0}
and
S^{n-1}
, respectively. We have natural maps of spaces

\displaystyle Y_{p}\times\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})

\displaystyle\to Y_{p}

\displaystyle\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0}),(\mathcal{C}_{1},\lambda_{1})\big)\times(\mathcal{C}_{2},\lambda_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big((\mathcal{C}_{0},\lambda_{0}),(\mathcal{C}_{1},\lambda_{1})\cdot(\mathcal{C}_{2},\lambda_{2})\big).

which are compatible with the face maps of
Y_{\bullet}
. As a result, we have an induced map
Y\times\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})\to Y
that gives
Y
the structure of a right
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-module.

We have a natural map

\alpha_{0}\colon\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})_{\leq 1}\times\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})

of
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-modules coming from the right
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-module map

m_{r}\colon\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})\times\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n}).

Since
\alpha_{0}\circ d_{0}=\alpha_{0}\circ d_{1}
, we have that
(Y_{\bullet},\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n}),\alpha_{0})
is an augmented semi-simplicial space and there is an induced map

\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon Y\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})

of
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-modules. We want to show that
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon Y\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is a weak homotopy equivalence. The map
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon Y\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
factors through
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
. Since
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
,
showing that the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon Y\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is a weak homotopy equivalence implies that the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon Y\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
We will show that the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon Y\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is a weak homotopy equivalence by using Gray’s theorem.

Theorem 4.25

(Gray’s theorem, e.g.
[
DI04
, Proposition 2.2]
)
.

Let
f\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
be a map of spaces and let
U
and
V
form an open cover of
X_{2}
. Suppose that the induced maps

f^{-1}(U)\to U,\qquad f^{-1}(V)\to V,\quad\text{ and }\quad f^{-1}(U\cap V)\to U\cap V

are all weak homotopy equivalences. Then
f\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
is also a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proposition 4.26
.

The map

\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon\|Y_{\bullet}\|\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})

is a weak homotopy equivalence of right
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
-modules.

Proof.

The image of
the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon\|Y_{\bullet}\|\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is contained in
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
.
Since the inclusion
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}\hookrightarrow\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is a homotopy equivalence by
Lemma

4.22
, it suffices to show that the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon\|Y_{\bullet}\|\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Let
U\subset\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
denote the subspace of configurations with no point in the closed disk
\bar{B}(0,1)
of radius
1
centered at the origin. Let
V\subset\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
denote the subspace of configurations where there is a unique closest point to the origin. The map
\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(U)\to U
is a weak homotopy equivalence because
U
is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty))
and
\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(U)
deformation retracts onto the subspace

\{((\{\emptyset\},0),(\mathcal{C},\lambda))\}\cong\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})\subset Y_{0},

which is homotopy equivalent to
\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty))
by
Lemma

4.7
.

One can show that
\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(V)\to V
is a weak homotopy equivalence by showing that the map is a fibration with contractible fibers as follows. Given
(\mathcal{C},\lambda)\in V
, we write
\mathcal{C}
as
\mathcal{C}=\{(q_{1},u_{1}),\ldots,(q_{k},u_{k})\}
such that
u_{1}<u_{2}\leq\ldots u_{k}
. Given any map
f\colon D^{r}\times[0,1]\to V
, we have that
f(x,t)\mathrel{{=}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}}(\mathcal{C}(x,t),\lambda(x,t))
satisfies that
u_{1}(x,t)<u_{i}(x,t)
for all
i=2,\ldots,k
and
(x,t)\in D^{r}\times[0,1]
. In addition, since
D^{r}\times[0,1]
is compact, there is some constant
K>0
such that
u_{i}(x,t)-u_{1}(x,t)\geq K
for all
i=2,\ldots,k
and
(x,t)\in D^{r}\times[0,1]
. As a result, given any commutative diagram,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{r}\times\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{r}\times[0,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|\alpha_{0}\|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

we can lift
f
to a map

\tilde{f}(x,t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}((\mathcal{C}_{0}(x,t),\lambda_{0}(x,t)),(\mathcal{C}_{1}(x,t),\lambda_{1}(x,t))\colon D^{r}\times[0,1]\to\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(V).

Roughly speaking, using the data of
f
and the fact that
u_{i}(x,t)-u_{1}(x,t)\geq K
for all
i>1
and
(x,t)\in D^{r}\times[0,1]
, we can continuously pick
\lambda_{0}(x,t)
to be an element in
\{0\}\cup(u_{1}(x,t),u_{2}(x,t))
. We can use the data of the underlying configuration
\mathcal{C}(x,t)
in
V
to continuously assign underlying configurations
\mathcal{C}_{0}(x,t)
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}(x,t)
in
\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(V)
. Finally, the data of
\lambda(x,t)
in
V
and
\lambda_{0}(x,t)
uniquely determines
\lambda_{1}(x,t)
.

We now show that the fiber of the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(V)\to V
is contractible. Suppose that
\zeta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathcal{C},\lambda)
is an element of
V
, with
\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(q_{1},u_{1}),\ldots,(q_{k},u_{k})\}
and
u_{1}<u_{2}\leq\ldots\leq u_{k}
.
The fiber
\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(\zeta)
is given by the geometric realization of the sub-semi-simplicial space of
Y_{\bullet}
whose image under
\alpha_{0}
is
\zeta
.
Denote this sub-semi-simplicial space by
Y_{\bullet}(\zeta)
. We have two cases to consider:
u_{1}=0
and
u_{1}>0
.

Suppose
u_{1}=0
. Then the space
Y_{1}(\zeta)
is empty, so
\|Y_{\bullet}(\zeta)\|=Y_{0}(\zeta)
. We have that
Y_{0}(\zeta)
is contractible because it is homeomorphic to an open interval or a half-open interval, depending on whether
q_{1}
is in the interior of
\bar{D}^{n}
or the boundary of it:

\displaystyle Y_{0}(\zeta)=\begin{cases}\big\{\big(((q_{1},0),\lambda_{0}),\text{sh}_{-\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{C}\setminus(q_{1},0),\lambda-\lambda_{0})\big):\lambda_{0}\in\left[0,u_{2}\right)\big\}&\text{if }q_{1}\in D^{n},\\
\big\{\big(((q_{1},0),\lambda_{0}),\text{sh}_{-\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{C}\setminus(q_{1},0),\lambda-\lambda_{0})\big):\lambda_{0}\in\left(0,u_{2}\right)\big\}:&\text{if }q_{1}\in\partial\bar{D}^{n}.\end{cases}

Suppose
u_{1}>0
. We have that
Y_{0}(\zeta)=F_{0}\cup F_{1}
, where

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle=\big\{\big((\{\emptyset\},\lambda_{0}),\text{sh}_{-\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{C},\lambda-\lambda_{0})\big):\lambda_{0}\in\left[0,u_{1}\right)\big\}\cong\left[0,u_{1}\right),\text{ and}

\displaystyle F_{1}

\displaystyle=\big\{\big(((q_{1},u_{1}),\lambda_{0}),\text{sh}_{-\lambda_{0}}(\mathcal{C}\setminus(q_{1},u_{1}),\lambda-\lambda_{0})\big):\lambda_{0}\in\left(u_{1},u_{2}\right)\big\}\cong(u_{1},u_{2}),

and
Y_{1}(\zeta)
is equal to
F_{1}
. The face maps
d_{0},d_{1}\colon Y_{1}(\zeta)\to Y_{0}(\zeta)
correspond to the constant map
(u_{1},u_{2})\to 0\in[0,u_{1})

and the identity map
(u_{1},u_{2})\to(u_{1},u_{2})
, respectively.
The geometric realization is homeomorphic to a half-open interval and so it is contractible.

Finally, by a similar argument, the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|^{-1}(U\cap V)\to U\cap V
is a weak homotopy equivalence. Therefore, by
Theorem

4.25
, the map
\|\alpha_{0}\|\colon\|Y_{\bullet}\|\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})^{\leq 1}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
∎

Remark 4.27
.

We have given a resolution of
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
without any motivation. We will now show that there is a completely conceptual explanation for how to come up with a guess for a resolution.

We have that
S\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});\mathbb{Z})
is a differential graded ring and
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
is a differential graded module over
S
. We want to find a resolution of
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
by free
S
-modules.
The ring
S
is augmented over
\mathbb{Z}
via the map which sends a chain
\sum_{i}n_{i}\sigma_{i}
to
\sum_{i}n_{i}
(here,
n_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\sigma_{i}\colon\bar{D}^{r}\to\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
is a singular
r
-simplex). Therefore, the hyper Tor groups

\text{Tor}^{S}_{*}(C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})

are defined. These hyper Tor groups provide lower bounds on the ranks of the free
S
-modules in a free resolution of
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
.
We can calculate these hyper Tor groups by calculating the hyper homology of the following bar construction

B_{\bullet}(C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});\mathbb{Z}),C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})

which is equivalent to
C_{*}(B_{\bullet}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n}),\text{MConf}(S^{n-1}),\text{pt});\mathbb{Z})
.
The homology of this double complex is isomorphic to the singular homology
H_{*}(\|B_{\bullet}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n}),\text{MConf}(S^{n-1}),\text{pt})\|;\mathbb{Z})
.

We can understand the space
\|B_{\bullet}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n}),\text{MConf}(S^{n-1}),\text{pt})\|
better by working with relative configuration spaces.
Let
L\subset X
be a closed subspace of a space
X
. The relative configuration space
\text{Conf}(X,L)
is the quotient space of
\text{Conf}(X)
by the equivalence relation which identifies two finite subsets
s
and
s_{1}
of
X
if
s\cap(X\setminus L)=s_{1}\cap(X\setminus L)
. The relative configuration space
\text{Conf}(D^{n},\text{pt})
is homotopy equivalent to a point, so

\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n},\text{pt})
is homotopy equivalent to a point by a relative version of
Lemma

4.7
. By a relative version of
Corollary

4.19
, the space

\|B_{\bullet}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n}),\text{MConf}(S^{n-1}),\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n},\text{pt}))\|

is weakly equivalent to the relative configuration space
\text{Conf}(S^{n},x_{0})
. By pushing all but one point of a configuration in
\text{Conf}(S^{n},x_{0})
to
x_{0}
, we have that
\text{Conf}(S^{n},x_{0})
is homotopy equivalent to
S^{n}
(this is a classic argument which happens in the construction of the scanning map–see
[
McD75
, p. 92–93]
).

Since the homology
H_{*}(S^{n})
is only nonzero in degrees
0
and
n
, this suggests we might be able to give a resolution of
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
from two free
S
-modules based on a suitable model of
S^{n}
. One such model for
S^{n}
is given by
the homotopy coequalizer
\text{hocoeq}(S^{n-1}\rightrightarrows S^{0})
.
This model for
S^{n}
can be viewed as a length two
semi-simplicial resolution

S^{n-1}\to S^{0}\rightrightarrows S^{n}

of
S^{n}
(the description mentioned in
Section

1.4
involves a different model for
S^{n}
, the cone on
S^{n-1}\to\text{pt}
, which we will not use). As a result, it is natural for us to guess that a resolution of
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});\mathbb{Z})
of length two can be built from free
S
-modules using
C_{*}(S^{n-1};\mathbb{Z})
and
C_{*}(S^{0};\mathbb{Z})
.

5.
Constructing Stabilization Maps

In this section, we construct various stabilization maps: first on
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
in
Section

5.1
, then on
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
in
Section

5.2
. Finally, in
Section

5.3
we define a stabilization map
t_{z,c}
on
\text{Conf}(M)
when
\left[z,e\right]=0
using a stabilization map on
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
from
Section

5.2
. When
M
is an open manifold, we also relate
t_{z,c}
to
t_{z}
at the end of
Section

5.3
in
Proposition

5.36
. Due to technicalities that only appear in the proof of this proposition, we will need to work with various stabilization maps on both
\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})
and
\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n})
.

5.1.
Stabilization Maps for the Configuration Space of a Cylinder

In this subsection, we construct a stabilization map
t_{z}^{l}
on
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
. In
Section

5.3
, we will use
t_{z}^{l}
to define a new stabilization map on
C_{*}(\text{Conf}(M);R)
when
\left[z,e\right]=0
. In order to obtain stability results for the configuration spaces of a closed manifold, we will need to show that when
M
is open, the new stabilization map and
t_{z}
are chain homotopic in a certain sense (see
Proposition

5.36
for a more precise statement). Our proof that they are chain homotopic will essentially involve showing that various stabilization maps on
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
are chain homotopic. For this reason, we begin this section by introducing a model for
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
.
Then we construct stabilization maps
s_{z}^{l}
and
s_{z}^{r}
on
this model
and relate them to
t_{z}^{l}
(see
Remark

5.7
for more of an explanation of why we introduce
s_{z}^{l}
and
s_{z}^{r}
instead of working only with
t_{z}^{l}
).
In
Section

5.2
, we will use these stabilization maps to construct stabilization maps on various models of
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)
.

Definition 5.1
.

Given spaces
W
and
X
, let

EZ\colon C_{*}(W;R)\otimes C_{*}(X;R)\to C_{*}(W\times X;R)

denote the Eilenberg–Zilber map.

The Eilenberg–Zilber map is a lax monoidal functor. As a result, if
M
is a topological monoid with monoid map
(-\cdot-)\colon M\times M\to M
then
C_{*}(M;R)
is a (differential-graded) ring under the map
(-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ\colon C_{*}(M;R)\otimes C_{*}(M;R)\to C_{*}(M;R)
. In addition, if
Z
is a right module over
M
with module map
m_{r}\colon M\times Z\to Z
, then
C_{*}(Z;R)
is a (differential graded) right module over
C_{*}(M;R)
under the map
(m_{r})_{*}\circ EZ\colon C_{*}(M;R)\otimes C_{*}(Z;R)\to C_{*}(Z;R)
.

View
R[x]
as a subring of
H_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
by identifying
x
with a generator of
H_{n-1}((S^{n-1}\times\{1/2\})\times\{1\};R)\cong H_{n-1}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1})_{1};R)
and extending to
x^{j}
by using the ring structure on
H_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)

(the ring structure on
H_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
comes from the ring structure on
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
).
The differential
\partial
on
R[x]\otimes_{R}C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)
sends
x^{j}\otimes y
to
x^{j}\otimes\partial y
.

Definition 5.2
.

Fix an embedding
f^{\prime}\colon D^{n}\to D^{n-1}\times(0,1)\subset S^{n-1}\times(0,1)
. Let
\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})

denote the map

\displaystyle\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})\colon\text{Conf}(D^{n})

\displaystyle\to\text{MConf}(S^{n-1}).

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\text{Conf}(f^{\prime})(\mathcal{C}),1)

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\alpha\colon R[x]\otimes_{R}C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
denote the map (see
Figure

9
)

\displaystyle\alpha\colon R[x]\otimes_{R}C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)

\displaystyle\to C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)

\displaystyle x^{j}\otimes y

\displaystyle\mapsto(-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ(x^{j}\otimes\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(y)).

Figure 9.

Lemma 5.4
.

The map
\alpha\colon R[x]\otimes_{R}C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)\to C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
is a chain homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Since
R[x]\otimes_{R}C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)
and
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
are bounded below chain complexes of free
R
-modules, it suffices to show that
\alpha
is a quasi-isomorphism.
By the universal coefficient theorem, it suffices to prove this for
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.

Note that

\text{Conf}(S^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})\simeq\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\text{pt})\simeq\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\text{pt})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Let
\text{Conf}_{k,l}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
denote the configuration space of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
consisting of
k+l
unordered distinct points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
k
of the points labelled red and
l
points labelled blue.
The space
\text{Conf}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\text{pt})\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the same as
\text{Conf}_{k,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We can think of
\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}\text{Conf}_{k,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as a subspace of the labelled configuration space
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n};S^{0}\bigvee S^{0})
(see
Definition

2.4
). The space
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n};S^{0}\bigvee S^{0})
is homotopy equivalent to
\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0})
.

Fix generators
b,r\in\tilde{H}_{0}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0};R)
corresponding to the two non-basepoint elements of
S^{0}\bigvee S^{0}
. Here, we think of
b
as the class of a blue point and
r
as the class of a red point. By
Theorem

2.6
,
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0});R)
can be calculated by first forming the set of Lie words on
b
and
r
. The free commutative algebra on the set of Lie words generates
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0});\mathbb{Q})
, and for
R=\mathbb{F}_{p}
, we obtain
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0});\mathbb{F}_{p})
by formally applying
Q^{s},\beta Q^{s},\xi,\beta\xi,
and
\zeta
. Note that by a generalization of the geometric interpretation of the Browder bracket (see
Remark

2.2
), we have that a Lie word containing more than one
b
(for example,
\left[b,\left[r,b\right]\right]
) corresponds to a homology class of
\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n};S^{0}\bigvee S^{0})
with at least two blue points. As a result, instead of considering the set of all Lie words on
b
and
r
, we only need to consider the set of Lie words containing at most one
b
to calculate
H_{*}(\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}\text{Conf}_{k,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
. If
w
is a Lie word containing only
r
’s, then by
Lemma

2.7

w
is either
r
or
\left[r,r\right]
, so we only need to figure out the set of all Lie words containing exactly one
b
.

Suppose that
w
is a Lie word containing exactly one
b
. We claim that we can assume that
w
is of the form
w=\left[r,\left[r,\ldots\left[r,b\right]\right]\ldots\right]
. We prove this claim by induction on
\text{weight}(w)
. If
y
is a Lie word of weight
1
and contains
b
, then by definition
w=b
. Suppose that the claim holds for all Lie words of weight
1,\ldots,m
. Let
w
be a word of weight
m+1
. Then
w=\left[x,y\right]
with
m+1=\text{weight}(x)+\text{weight}(y)
and
b
contained exactly once in
x
or
y
. Since the Browder bracket is symmetric up to a sign in
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0});R)
(Property
2
of the Browder bracket), without loss of generality we can assume that
x
is a Lie word containing only
r
’s and
y
is a Lie word containing exactly one
b
. Since
y
is a Lie word of weight at most
m
it is of the form
y=\left[r,\left[r,\ldots\left[r,b\right]\right]\ldots\right]
. For simplicity, we write
y
as
y=\left[r,z\right]
. Since
x
is a Lie word containing only
r
’s, by
Lemma

2.7
, we can assume that
x
is either
r
or
\left[r,r\right]
. If
x
is
r
, then the claim holds. Otherwise, by the Jacobi identity for the Browder bracket (Property
3
), we have that

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\left[\left[r,r\right],\left[r,z\right]\right]\pm\left[r,\left[z,\left[r,r\right]\right]\right]\pm\left[z,\left[\left[r,r\right],r\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w\pm\left[r,\left[z,\left[r,r\right]\right]\right]\pm\left[z,\left[\left[r,r\right],r\right]\right].

By induction, since
\left[z,\left[r,r\right]\right]
is a Lie word of weight at most
m
, it is of the form
\left[r,\left[r,\ldots\left[r,b\right]\right]\ldots\right]
. Since
\left[\left[r,r\right],r\right]=0
(Property
3
), we have that
w
is of the desired form.

We now describe how homology operations affect Lie words containing only
b
’s and
r
’s. We can think of
\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0})
as an
E_{n}
-algebra graded over
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, i.e.

\mathbf{E_{n}}(S^{0}\bigvee S^{0})=\bigsqcup_{(k,l)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}A_{k,l},

where
A_{k,l}
is homotopy equivalent
to
\text{Conf}_{k,l}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Similar to how the homology operations
Q^{s},\beta Q^{s},\xi,\beta\xi,
and
\zeta
act on the homology an
E_{n}
-algebra graded over
\mathbb{N}
, these homology operations send classes in
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{k,l}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
to classes in
H_{*}(\text{Conf}_{pk,pl}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{p})
. As a result, the only way that applying one of the homology operations
Q^{s},\beta Q^{s},\xi,\beta\xi,
or
\zeta
to a Lie word containing at most one
b
can be a homology class in
\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}\text{Conf}_{k,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is if the Lie word contains only
r
’s.

In Cohen’s calculation of
H_{*}(\mathbf{E_{n}}(X);\mathbb{F}_{p})
(see page 227 of
[
CLM76
]
), the constraint on
which formal applications of the homology operations
Q^{s},\beta Q^{s},\xi,\beta\xi,
and
\zeta
are allowed to act a Lie word
w

depends only on
n
and the homological degrees of
w
and
p
. Therefore, we have an explicit isomorphism from
R[x]\otimes H_{*}(\text{Conf}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
to
H_{*}(\bigsqcup_{k\geq 0}\text{Conf}_{k,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n});R)
given by the map sending
x^{i}\otimes\sigma
to
\left[r,\left[r,\ldots\left[r,b\right]\right]\ldots\right]\sigma
, where
r
appears
i
times in
\left[r,\left[r,\ldots\left[r,b\right]\right]\ldots\right]
, and extending linearly. Since this isomorphism corresponds to the map on homology induced by
\alpha
,

\alpha_{*}\colon R[x]\otimes_{R}H_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)\to H_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R),

we have that
\alpha
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Now we construct stabilization maps and compare them to each other.

Definition 5.5
.

Given a class
z\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)
, let

t_{z}^{l}\colon C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)\to C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)

denote the map
(-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ\big(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(z)\otimes-\big)
.

Definition 5.6
.

Given a positive integer
i
, let
\lambda^{i}(e,z)
denote
\left[e,\left[e,\ldots\left[e,z\right]\right]\ldots\right]
, where
e
appears in the iterated Browder bracket
i
times, and let
\lambda^{0}(e,z)
denote
z
.
Let
s^{l}_{z},s^{r}_{z}\colon R[x]\otimes C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)\to R[x]\otimes C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)
denote the maps

\displaystyle s^{l}_{z}\colon R[x]\otimes C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)

\displaystyle\to R[x]\otimes C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R),

\displaystyle x^{j}\otimes y

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{j}\binom{j}{i}x^{j-i}\otimes(m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i}(e,z))\otimes y)

\displaystyle s^{r}_{z}\colon R[x]\otimes C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)

\displaystyle\to R[x]\otimes C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R).

\displaystyle x^{j}\otimes y

\displaystyle\mapsto x^{j}\otimes(m_{r})_{*}\circ EZ(y\otimes\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(z))

Remark 5.7
.

We think of
s_{z}^{l}
and
s_{z}^{r}
as representing stabilizing
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
by putting
z
on the “left” and “right” side of
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
, respectively. For technical reasons, in the proof of
Proposition

5.36
we will need that these two ways of stabilizing
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
are chain homotopic when
\left[z,e\right]=0
.
In that proof, we will also need something stronger: roughly speaking, the homotopy will also need to be a map of right
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(\bar{D}^{n-1});R)
-modules (
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
is a right
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(\bar{D}^{n-1});R)
-module since
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
is a right module over itself and
\bar{D}^{n-1}\subset S^{n-1}
).

An advantage of working with
R[x]\otimes C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)
is that we can construct a chain homotopy between
s_{z}^{l}
and
s_{z}^{r}
when
\left[z,e\right]=0

explicitly
and algebraically. This will ensure that the homotopy that we need in the proof of
Proposition

5.36
will also be a map of right
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(\bar{D}^{n-1});R)
-modules.
We still need to work with
t_{z}^{l}
because we use it to define a stabilization map on
C_{*}(\text{Mod-Conf}^{\,1}_{S^{n-1}}(\bar{D}^{n});R)
that is also a map of right
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
-modules (we cannot use
s_{z}^{l}
and
s_{z}^{r}
because they are not maps of right
C_{*}(\text{MConf}(S^{n-1});R)
-modules).

Lemma 5.8
.

The maps
t_{z}^{l}\circ\alpha
and
\alpha\circ s_{z}^{l}
are chain homotopic.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\alpha\circ s^{l}_{z}(x^{j}\otimes y)

\displaystyle=\alpha\big(\sum_{i=0}^{j}\binom{j}{i}x^{j-i}\otimes\big((m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i}(e,z))\otimes y)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=(-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ\big(\sum_{i=0}^{j}\binom{j}{i}x^{j-i}\otimes\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}\big((m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i}(e,z))\otimes y)\big)\big)

and

\displaystyle t^{l}_{z}\circ\alpha(x^{j}\otimes y)

\displaystyle=t^{l}_{z}\big((-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ\big(x^{j}\otimes\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(y)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=(-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ\big(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(z)\otimes\big((-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ(x^{j}\otimes\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(y))\big)\big).

We will prove that they are chain homotopic by recursion on
j
. We have that
\alpha\circ s^{l}_{z}(1\otimes-)
and
t^{l}_{z}\circ\alpha(1\otimes-)
are chain homotopic because the difference between these two maps corresponds to a different choice of orientation-preserving embeddings
\mathbb{R}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{R}^{n}\hookrightarrow S^{n-1}\times(0,\infty)
and the space of such embedding is path-connected.

Suppose now that
j>0
.
We have that

\displaystyle s^{l}_{z}(x^{j}\otimes-)=

\displaystyle(-\cdot-)_{*}\Big((x\otimes(\emptyset,0))\otimes\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\binom{j-1}{i}x^{j-1-i}\otimes\big((m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i}(e,z))\otimes-)\big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\binom{j-1}{i}x^{j-1-i}\otimes((m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i}(e,\left[e,z\right]))\otimes-))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-\cdot-)_{*}\big((x\otimes(\emptyset,0))\otimes s^{l}_{z}(x^{j-1}\otimes-)\big)+s^{l}_{\left[e,z\right]}(x^{j-1}\otimes-)

since
\binom{j-1}{i}+\binom{j-1}{i-1}=\binom{j}{i}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\alpha\circ s^{l}_{z}(x^{j}\otimes-)

\displaystyle=\alpha\big((-\cdot-)_{*}\big((x\otimes(\emptyset,0))\otimes s^{l}_{z}(x^{j-1}\otimes-)\big)+s^{l}_{\left[e,z\right]}(x^{j-1}\otimes-)\big)

\displaystyle=(-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ(x\otimes(\alpha\circ s^{l}_{z}(x^{j-1}\otimes-)))+(\alpha\circ s^{l}_{\left[e,z\right]})(x^{j-1}\otimes-)

Similarly, by
Figure

10

we have that

t_{z}^{l}\circ\alpha(x^{j}\otimes-)\simeq(-\cdot-)_{*}\circ EZ(x\otimes(t_{z}^{l}\circ\alpha(x^{j-1}\otimes-)))+(t_{\left[e,z\right]}^{l}\circ\alpha)(x^{j-1}\otimes-).

Figure 10.

By recursion on
j
, it follows that

t_{z}^{l}\circ\alpha(x^{j}\otimes-)
is chain homotopic to
\alpha\circ s_{z}^{l}(x^{j}\otimes-)
.
∎

Proposition 5.9
.

Suppose that
z\in H_{*}(\text{Conf}(D^{n});R)
is a homology class such that the Browder bracket
\left[z,e\right]=0
. Then the maps
s^{l}_{z}
and
s^{r}_{z}
are chain homotopic.

Proof.

We have that

s^{l}_{z}(x^{j}\otimes y)=\sum_{i=0}^{j}\binom{j}{i}x^{j-i}\otimes(m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i}(e,z))\otimes y).

Since the map
(x^{j}\otimes y)\mapsto x^{j}\otimes(m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(z)\otimes y)
is homotopic to
s^{r}_{z}(x^{j}\otimes y)
, it suffices to show that the map

F(x^{j}\otimes y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{j}\binom{j}{i}x^{j-i}\otimes(m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i}(e,z))\otimes y)

is null-homotopic. Since
i
is positive and
\lambda^{i}(e,z)=\lambda^{i-1}(e,\left[e,z\right])
, we have that

F(x^{j}\otimes y)=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\binom{j}{i}x^{j-i}\otimes(m_{l})_{*}\circ EZ(\text{MConf}(f^{\prime})_{*}(\lambda^{i-1}(e,\left[e,z\right]))\otimes y).

Fix a chain
